Estimate your take-home pay with our [Salary Calculator for Bristol](https://www.numbeo.com/salary-calculator/in/Bristol).\n\n![](https://www.numbeo.com/images/baseline-comment.svg)Leave a comment:\n\n3 Comments so far\n\nAnonymous on Aug 27, 2026:\n\nAs a Mental Health Worker, my annual salary is £26,056.   \n   \n Lets do the maths:   \n   \n Mental Health Worker Salary = £26,056 per year   \n   \n £26,056 ÷ (37.5 × 52)   \n = £13.36 per hour   \n   \n National Living Wage = £12.21 per hour   \n   \n Difference:   \n £13.36 - £12.21   \n = £1.15 per hour   \n   \n Percentage above minimum wage:   \n (£1.15 ÷ £12.21) × 100   \n = 9.4%   \n   \n So:   \n   \n Mental Health Worker   \n = Minimum Wage + 9.4%   \n   \n Yet the role includes:   \n + Managing risk   \n + Safeguarding vulnerable adults   \n + Crisis intervention   \n + Lone working   \n + Community outreach   \n + Complex caseloads   \n + Extensive documentation   \n + Supporting people through significant mental health challenges   \n   \n Meanwhile, the expense model shows:   \n   \n Rent = 33.0%   \n Food = 22.9%   \n Transport = 10.7%   \n Utilities = 6.5%   \n -----------------------   \n Essentials = 73.1%   \n   \n 100% - 73.1%   \n = 26.9%   \n   \n £26,056 × 26.9%   \n ≈ £7,009 per year   \n ≈ £584 per month   \n   \n Remaining £584 per month must then cover:   \n - Council tax   \n - Phone and internet   \n - Insurance   \n - Clothing   \n - Household costs   \n - Emergencies   \n - Savings   \n - Social life   \n - Any unexpected expenses   \n   \n The equation becomes:   \n   \n High Responsibility   \n + High Cost of Living   \n + Expensive Public Transport   \n + Supporting Vulnerable People   \n + Lone Working   \n + Crisis Management   \n =   \n Minimum Wage + 9.4%   \n   \n The maths doesn't reflect the demands of the role nor in line with current cost of living\n\nAnonymous on May 05, 2022:\n\nRice is 45p in Tescos for 1 kg https://www.tesco.com/groceries/en-GB/products/300843799   \n As far as I can see that is third world levels of cheapness according to this site.\n\nJesiotr on Mar 11, 2021:\n\nI was living in Bristol 2016-2018 and now I am close by in somerset. With 33k salary at that time, I was struggling to save any money. I had a normal 2 bedroom house and I am not a big spender. I would say for Bristol the minimum salary for a family is 40k. The county includes most of the Bristol conurbation, but the urban area extends into South Gloucestershire, Bath and North East Somerset, and North Somerset. |\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/Clifton_Suspension_Bridge_and_the_Observatory_in_Bristol%2C_England.jpg/330px-Clifton_Suspension_Bridge_and_the_Observatory_in_Bristol%2C_England.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Harbour_View%2C_Bristol_-_geograph.org.uk_-_5352614.jpg/250px-Harbour_View%2C_Bristol_-_geograph.org.uk_-_5352614.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/2023-09-15_Bristol_Cathedral_01.jpg/250px-2023-09-15_Bristol_Cathedral_01.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/Victoria_Rooms_in_Bristol._-_geograph.org.uk_-_3690458.jpg/250px-Victoria_Rooms_in_Bristol._-_geograph.org.uk_-_3690458.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/The_Centre%2C_Bristol%2C_looking_towards_Clare_Street.jpg/250px-The_Centre%2C_Bristol%2C_looking_towards_Clare_Street.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Bristol_University_-_geograph.org.uk_-_5266015.jpg/120px-Bristol_University_-_geograph.org.uk_-_5266015.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/Brandon%2C_Bristol%2C_UK_-_panoramio.jpg/250px-Brandon%2C_Bristol%2C_UK_-_panoramio.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![Coat of arms of Bristol](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Arms_of_Bristol_City_Council.svg/120px-Arms_of_Bristol_City_Council.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![Bristol shown within England](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/Bristol_UK_location_map.svg/250px-Bristol_UK_location_map.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\n**Bristol** ([/ˈbrɪstəl/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English \"Help:IPA/English\") [ⓘ](/wiki/File:En-uk-Bristol.ogg \"File:En-uk-Bristol.ogg\")) is a [city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_status_in_the_United_Kingdom \"City status in the United Kingdom\") and [ceremonial county](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceremonial_county \"Ceremonial county\") in [South West England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_West_England \"South West England\"). It is on the [River Avon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Avon,_Bristol \"River Avon, Bristol\"), and bordered by [Gloucestershire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gloucestershire \"Gloucestershire\") to the north and [Somerset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Somerset \"Somerset\") to the south, with a short coastline on the [Bristol Channel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Channel \"Bristol Channel\") to the west. The county includes most of the [Bristol conurbation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Bristol \"Greater Bristol\"), but the urban area extends into [South Gloucestershire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Gloucestershire \"South Gloucestershire\"), [Bath and North East Somerset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bath_and_North_East_Somerset \"Bath and North East Somerset\"), and [North Somerset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Somerset \"North Somerset\").\n\nThe county is almost entirely urbanised and had an estimated population of 494,399 in 2024, making Bristol the most populous city in the region.[[6]](#cite_note-6)[[7]](#cite_note-7) For local government purposes the county is a [unitary authority area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_authorities_of_England \"Unitary authorities of England\") governed by [Bristol City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_City_Council \"Bristol City Council\"). The council is a member of the [West of England Combined Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_of_England_Combined_Authority \"West of England Combined Authority\"), which allows it to collaborate with [South Gloucestershire Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Gloucestershire_Council \"South Gloucestershire Council\") and [Bath and North East Somerset Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bath_and_North_East_Somerset_Council \"Bath and North East Somerset Council\").\n\n[Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age \"Iron Age\") [hillforts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillfort \"Hillfort\") and [Roman villas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_villa \"Roman villa\") were built near the [confluence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confluence \"Confluence\") of the rivers [Frome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Frome,_Bristol \"River Frome, Bristol\") and Avon. Bristol received a [royal charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_charter \"Royal charter\") in 1155, but was [historically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historic_counties_of_England \"Historic counties of England\") divided between Gloucestershire and Somerset until it became a [county corporate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_corporate \"County corporate\") in 1373. From the 13th to the 18th centuries, Bristol was among the top three English cities, after London, in tax receipts. A major port, Bristol was a starting place for early voyages of exploration to the New World. At the height of the [Bristol slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_slave_trade \"Bristol slave trade\"), from 1700 to 1807, more than 2,000 slave ships carried an estimated 500,000 people from Africa to slavery in the Americas. The [Port of Bristol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Bristol \"Port of Bristol\") has since moved from [Bristol Harbour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Harbour \"Bristol Harbour\") in the city centre to the [Severn Estuary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Severn_Estuary \"Severn Estuary\") at [Avonmouth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avonmouth \"Avonmouth\") and [Royal Portbury Dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Portbury_Dock \"Royal Portbury Dock\").\n\nThe city's modern economy is built on the creative media, electronics, and [aerospace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerospace \"Aerospace\") industries; the city-centre docks have been redeveloped as cultural and heritage centres. There are a variety of artistic and sporting organisations and venues, including the [Royal West of England Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_West_of_England_Academy \"Royal West of England Academy\"), the [Arnolfini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnolfini,_Bristol \"Arnolfini, Bristol\"), [Ashton Gate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashton_Gate_Stadium \"Ashton Gate Stadium\") and the [Memorial Ground](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorial_Ground \"Memorial Ground\"). The city has two universities: the [University of Bristol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Bristol \"University of Bristol\") and the [University of the West of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_the_West_of_England \"University of the West of England\") (UWE Bristol). It is connected to the world by [Bristol Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Airport \"Bristol Airport\"); to the rest of [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Britain \"Great Britain\") via [Bristol Temple Meads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Temple_Meads_railway_station \"Bristol Temple Meads railway station\") and [Bristol Parkway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Parkway_railway_station \"Bristol Parkway railway station\") mainline rail stations; and by road by both the south-west to [West Midlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Midlands_(region) \"West Midlands (region)\") [M5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M5_motorway \"M5 motorway\") and the London to South Wales [M4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M4_motorway \"M4 motorway\") (which connect to the city centre by the [Portway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portway,_Bristol \"Portway, Bristol\") and [M32](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M32_motorway \"M32 motorway\")).\n\n## Toponymy\n\nThe name derives from the [Old English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_English \"Old English\") *Brycgstow*, meaning \"assembly place by the bridge\" or simply \"site of the bridge\", presumably in reference to a crossing over the Avon.[[8]](#cite_note-8)[[9]](#cite_note-9) The final 'L' is an unetymological addition that first appears in the 12th century, which may be due to the unique \"Terminal L\" found in the [Bristol dialect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_in_Bristol#Dialect \"Culture in Bristol\"). An older form of the name survives as the surname [Bristow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristow_(surname) \"Bristow (surname)\"), which derives from the city.[[10]](#cite_note-10)\n\n## History\n\n[Archaeological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaeological \"Archaeological\") finds, including flint tools believed to be between 300,000 and 126,000 years old made with the [Levallois technique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levallois_technique \"Levallois technique\"), indicate the presence of [Neanderthals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neanderthal \"Neanderthal\") in the [Shirehampton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shirehampton \"Shirehampton\") and [St Annes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brislington_West_(ward) \"Brislington West (ward)\") areas of Bristol during the [Middle Palaeolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Palaeolithic \"Middle Palaeolithic\").[[11]](#cite_note-11) [Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age \"Iron Age\") [hill forts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hill_fort \"Hill fort\") near the city are at [Leigh Woods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leigh_Woods \"Leigh Woods\") and [Clifton Down](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clifton_Down \"Clifton Down\"), on the side of the [Avon Gorge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avon_Gorge \"Avon Gorge\"), and on [Kings Weston Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kings_Weston_Hill \"Kings Weston Hill\") near [Henbury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henbury,_Bristol \"Henbury, Bristol\").[[12]](#cite_note-12) A [Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Britain \"Roman Britain\") port, *Portus Abonae* (Avonport) – abbreviated to *Abona* in the [Antonine Itinerary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonine_Itinerary \"Antonine Itinerary\"),[[13]](#cite_note-13) existed at what is now [Sea Mills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_Mills,_Bristol \"Sea Mills, Bristol\") (connected to [Bath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bath,_Somerset \"Bath, Somerset\") and [Gloucester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gloucester \"Gloucester\") by [Roman roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_road \"Roman road\")); another settlement was at the present-day [Inns Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filwood_(ward)#Inns_Court \"Filwood (ward)\"). Isolated [Roman villas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_villa \"Roman villa\") and small [forts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castra \"Castra\") and settlements were also scattered throughout the area.[[14]](#cite_note-14)\n\n### Middle Ages\n\nBristol was founded by 1000; by about 1020, it was a trading centre with a [mint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mint_(coin) \"Mint (coin)\") producing silver pennies bearing its name.[[15]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTELobelCarus-Wilson19752–3-15) By 1067, Brycgstow was a well-fortified *[burh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burh \"Burh\")*, and that year the townsmen beat back a raiding party from Ireland led by three of [Harold Godwinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harold_Godwinson \"Harold Godwinson\")'s sons.[[15]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTELobelCarus-Wilson19752–3-15) Under [Norman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normans \"Normans\") rule, the town had one of the strongest [castles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Castle \"Bristol Castle\") in [southern England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_England \"Southern England\").[[16]](#cite_note-16) Bristol was the place of exile for [Diarmait Mac Murchada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diarmait_Mac_Murchada \"Diarmait Mac Murchada\"), the Irish [king of Leinster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Leinster \"King of Leinster\"), after being overthrown. The Bristol merchants subsequently played a prominent role in funding [Richard Strongbow de Clare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Strongbow_de_Clare \"Richard Strongbow de Clare\") and the [Norman invasion of Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman_invasion_of_Ireland \"Norman invasion of Ireland\").[[17]](#cite_note-17)\n\n![Fifteenth-century pictorial map of Bristol, radiating from the town centre](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/d/d0/Robert_Ricart%27s_map_of_Bristol.png/250px-Robert_Ricart%27s_map_of_Bristol.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nThe port developed in the 11th century around the confluence of the [Rivers Frome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Frome,_Bristol \"River Frome, Bristol\") and [Avon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Avon_(Bristol) \"River Avon (Bristol)\"), adjacent to [Bristol Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Bridge \"Bristol Bridge\") just outside the town walls.[[19]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrace197613–15-19) By the 12th century, there was an important [Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_England \"History of the Jews in England\") community in Bristol which survived through to the late 13th century when all Jews were [expelled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Expulsion \"Edict of Expulsion\") from England.[[20]](#cite_note-20) The stone bridge built in 1247 was replaced by the current bridge during the 1760s.[[21]](#cite_note-21) The town incorporated neighbouring suburbs and became a [county](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_corporate \"County corporate\") in 1373,[[22]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTELiddy200513-22) the first town in England to be given this status.[[23]](#cite_note-23)[[24]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayfield198517–23-24)[[25]](#cite_note-25) During this period, Bristol became a shipbuilding and manufacturing centre.[[26]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTECarus-Wilson1933183–246-26) By the 14th century, Bristol, [York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/York \"York\") and [Norwich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norwich \"Norwich\") were England's largest [medieval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval \"Medieval\") towns after London.[[27]](#cite_note-27) One-third to one-half of the population died in the [Black Death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Death \"Black Death\") of 1348–49,[[28]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCulloch1839398–399-28) which checked population growth, and its population remained between 10,000 and 12,000 for most of the 15th and 16th centuries.[[29]](#cite_note-29)\n\n### 15th and 16th centuries\n\n![A stone-built Victorian Gothic building with two square towers and a central arched entrance underneath a circular ornate window. A Victorian street lamp stands in front of the building and on the right part of a leafless tree, with blue skies behind.](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Bristol.cathedral.west.front.arp.jpg/250px-Bristol.cathedral.west.front.arp.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nDuring the 15th century, Bristol was the second most important port in the country, trading with Ireland,[[30]](#cite_note-30) Iceland[[31]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTECarus-Wilson1933155–182-31) and [Gascony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gascony \"Gascony\").[[26]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTECarus-Wilson1933183–246-26) It was the starting point for many voyages, including [Robert Sturmy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Sturmy \"Robert Sturmy\")'s (1457–58) unsuccessful attempt to break the Italian [monopoly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monopoly \"Monopoly\") of Eastern Mediterranean trade.[[32]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJenks20061-32) New exploration voyages were launched by Venetian [John Cabot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Cabot \"John Cabot\"), who in 1497 made landfall in North America.[[33]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJonesCondon2016-33) A 1499 voyage, led by merchant [William Weston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Weston_(explorer) \"William Weston (explorer)\") of Bristol, was the first expedition commanded by an Englishman to [North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_America \"North America\").[[34]](#cite_note-34) During the first decade of the 16th century, Bristol's merchants undertook a series of exploration voyages to North America and even founded a commercial organisation, 'The Company Adventurers to the New Found Land', to assist their endeavours.[[35]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJonesCondon201657–70-35) However, they seem to have lost interest in North America after 1509, having incurred great expenses and made little profit.\n\nDuring the 16th century, Bristol merchants concentrated on developing trade with Spain and its American colonies.[[36]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnell-Smith195410-36) This included the [smuggling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smuggling \"Smuggling\") of prohibited goods, such as food and guns, to Iberia[[37]](#cite_note-37) during the [Anglo-Spanish War (1585–1604)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Spanish_War_(1585–1604) \"Anglo-Spanish War (1585–1604)\").[[38]](#cite_note-38) Bristol's illicit trade grew enormously after 1558, becoming integral to its economy.[[39]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJones2012-39)\n\nThe original [Diocese of Bristol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocese_of_Bristol \"Diocese of Bristol\") was founded in 1542,[[40]](#cite_note-40) when the former [Abbey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbey \"Abbey\") of [St. Augustine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augustine_of_Canterbury \"Augustine of Canterbury\") (founded by [Robert Fitzharding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Fitzharding \"Robert Fitzharding\") four hundred years earlier)[[41]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBettey19961–5-41) became [Bristol Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Cathedral \"Bristol Cathedral\"). Bristol also gained [city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_status_in_the_United_Kingdom \"City status in the United Kingdom\") status that year.[[42]](#cite_note-42)\n\n### 17th and 18th centuries\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/Charles_E._Flower%2C_The_Old_Dutch_House%2C_Bristol.jpg/250px-Charles_E._Flower%2C_The_Old_Dutch_House%2C_Bristol.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nIn the 1640s, during the [English Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Civil_War \"English Civil War\"), [the city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_in_the_English_Civil_War \"Bristol in the English Civil War\") was occupied by [Royalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cavalier \"Cavalier\"), who built the [Royal Fort House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Fort_House \"Royal Fort House\") on the site of an earlier [Parliamentarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roundhead \"Roundhead\") stronghold.[[43]](#cite_note-43)\n\nFishermen from Bristol, who had fished the [Grand Banks of Newfoundland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Banks_of_Newfoundland \"Grand Banks of Newfoundland\") since the 16th century,[[44]](#cite_note-44) began settling Newfoundland permanently in larger numbers during the 17th century, establishing colonies at [Bristol's Hope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol's_Hope \"Bristol's Hope\") and [Cuper's Cove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuper's_Cove \"Cuper's Cove\"). Growth of the city and trade came with the rise of England's [American colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_colonization_of_the_Americas \"European colonization of the Americas\") in the 17th century. Bristol's location on the west side of Great Britain gave its ships an advantage in sailing to and from the New World, and the city's merchants made the most of it, with the city becoming one of the two leading outports in all of England by the middle of the 18th century.[[45]](#cite_note-45) Bristol was the slave capital of England: In 1755, it had the largest number of slave traders in the country with 237, as against London's 147.[[46]](#cite_note-46) It was a major supplier of slaves to [South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Carolina \"South Carolina\") before 1750.[[47]](#cite_note-47)\n\n![ An engraving showing at the top a sailing ship and paddle steamer in a harbour, with sheds and a church spire. On either side arched gateways, all above a scroll with the word \"Bristol\". Below a street scene showing pedestrians and a horse-drawn carriage outside a large ornate building with a colonnade and arched windows above. A grand staircase with two figures ascending and other figures on a balcony. A caption reading \"Exterior, Colston Hall\" and Staircase, Colston Hall\". Below, two street scenes and a view of a large stone building with flying buttresses and a square tower, with the caption \"Bristol cathedral\". At the bottom views of a church interior, a cloister with a man mowing grass and archways with two men in conversation.](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Bristol_1873.png/250px-Bristol_1873.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nThe 18th century saw an expansion of Bristol's population (45,000 in 1750)[[48]](#cite_note-48) and its role in the [Atlantic trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_slave_trade \"Atlantic slave trade\") in Africans taken for [slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery \"Slavery\") to the Americas. Bristol and later [Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool \"Liverpool\") became centres of the [Triangular Trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triangular_Trade \"Triangular Trade\").[[49]](#cite_note-49) Manufactured goods were shipped to West Africa and exchanged for Africans; the enslaved captives were transported across the Atlantic to the Americas in the [Middle Passage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Passage \"Middle Passage\") under brutal conditions.[[50]](#cite_note-nmm-50) Plantation goods such as sugar, tobacco, rum, rice, cotton and a few slaves (sold to the aristocracy as house servants) returned across the Atlantic to England.[[50]](#cite_note-nmm-50) Some household slaves were baptised in the hope this would lead them to be freed. The [Somersett Case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Somersett_Case \"Somersett Case\") of 1772 clarified that slavery was illegal in England.[[51]](#cite_note-51) At the height of the [Bristol slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_slave_trade \"Bristol slave trade\") from 1700 to 1807, more than 2,000 slave ships carried a conservatively estimated 500,000 people from Africa to slavery in the Americas.[[52]](#cite_note-52)\n\nIn 1739, [John Wesley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Wesley \"John Wesley\") founded the first [Methodist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methodist \"Methodist\") chapel, the [New Room](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Room \"New Room\"), in Bristol.[[53]](#cite_note-53) Wesley, along with his brother [Charles Wesley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Wesley \"Charles Wesley\") and [George Whitefield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Whitefield \"George Whitefield\"), preached to large congregations in Bristol and the neighbouring village of [Kingswood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingswood,_South_Gloucestershire \"Kingswood, South Gloucestershire\"), often in the open air.[[54]](#cite_note-54)[[55]](#cite_note-55)\n\nWesley published a pamphlet on slavery, titled *Thoughts Upon Slavery,* in 1774[[56]](#cite_note-56) and the [Society of Friends](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society_of_Friends \"Society of Friends\") began lobbying against slavery in Bristol in 1783. The city's scions remained nonetheless strongly anti-abolitionist. [Thomas Clarkson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Clarkson \"Thomas Clarkson\") came to Bristol to study the slave trade and gained access to the [Society of Merchant Venturers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society_of_Merchant_Venturers \"Society of Merchant Venturers\") records.[[57]](#cite_note-BRS#1-57) One of his contacts was the owner of the [Seven Stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Stars,_Bristol \"Seven Stars, Bristol\") [public house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_house \"Public house\"), who boarded sailors Clarkson sought to meet. Through these sailors he was able to observe how slaver captains and first mates \"plied and stupefied seamen with drink\" to sign them up.[[57]](#cite_note-BRS#1-57)[[58]](#cite_note-58) Other informants included ship surgeons and seamen seeking redress. When [William Wilberforce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Wilberforce \"William Wilberforce\") began his parliamentary abolition campaign on 12 May 1788, he recalled the history of the [Irish slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_slave_trade \"Irish slave trade\") from Bristol, which he provocatively claimed continued into the reign of [Henry VII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_VII_of_England \"Henry VII of England\").[[57]](#cite_note-BRS#1-57) [Hannah More](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hannah_More \"Hannah More\"), originally from Bristol, and a good friend of both Wilberforce and Clarkson, published \"Slavery, A Poem\" in 1788, just as Wilberforce began his parliamentary campaign.[[59]](#cite_note-59) His major speech on 2 April 1792 likewise described the Bristol slave trade specifically, and led to the arrest, trial and subsequent acquittal of a local slaver captain named Kimber.[[57]](#cite_note-BRS#1-57)\n\n### 19th century\n\nThe city was associated with Victorian engineer [Isambard Kingdom Brunel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isambard_Kingdom_Brunel \"Isambard Kingdom Brunel\"), who designed the [Great Western Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Western_Railway \"Great Western Railway\") between Bristol and [London Paddington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Paddington \"London Paddington\"), two pioneering Bristol-built oceangoing [steamships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steamboat \"Steamboat\") ([SS *Great Britain*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SS_Great_Britain \"SS Great Britain\") and [SS *Great Western*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SS_Great_Western \"SS Great Western\")), and the [Clifton Suspension Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clifton_Suspension_Bridge \"Clifton Suspension Bridge\"). The new railway replaced the [Kennet and Avon Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kennet_and_Avon_Canal \"Kennet and Avon Canal\"), which had fully opened in 1810, as the main route for the transport of goods between Bristol and London.[[60]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClew197079–80-60) Competition from Liverpool (beginning around 1760), disruptions of maritime commerce due to war with France (1793) and the abolition of the slave trade (1807) contributed to Bristol's failure to keep pace with the newer manufacturing centres of [Northern England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_England \"Northern England\") and the [West Midlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Midlands_(region) \"West Midlands (region)\"). The tidal Avon Gorge, which had secured the port during the Middle Ages, had become a liability. An 1804–09 plan to improve the city's port with a [floating harbour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Harbour \"Bristol Harbour\") designed by [William Jessop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Jessop \"William Jessop\") was a costly error, requiring high harbour fees.[[61]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuchananCossons196932–33-61)\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4d/Bristol_Harbour_%28St_Stephen%27s_Church%2C_St_Augustine_the_Less_Church%2C_Bristol_Cathedral%29%2C_BRO_Picbox-7-PBA-22%2C_1250x1250.jpg/250px-Bristol_Harbour_%28St_Stephen%27s_Church%2C_St_Augustine_the_Less_Church%2C_Bristol_Cathedral%29%2C_BRO_Picbox-7-PBA-22%2C_1250x1250.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nDuring the 19th century, [Samuel Plimsoll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Plimsoll \"Samuel Plimsoll\"), known as \"the sailor's friend\", campaigned to make the seas safer; shocked by overloaded vessels, he successfully fought for a compulsory [load line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Load_line_(vessel) \"Load line (vessel)\") on ships.[[62]](#cite_note-62)\n\nBy 1867, ships were getting larger and the meanders in the river Avon prevented boats over 300 ft (90 m) from reaching the harbour, resulting in falling trade.[[63]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTECoules2006194–195-63) The port facilities were migrating downstream to [Avonmouth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avonmouth \"Avonmouth\") and new industrial complexes were founded there.[[64]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuchananCossons1969224–225-64) Some of the traditional industries including copper and brass manufacture went into decline,[[65]](#cite_note-65) but the import and processing of [tobacco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tobacco \"Tobacco\") flourished with the expansion of the [W.D. & H.O. Wills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W.D._&_H.O._Wills \"W.D. & H.O. Wills\") business.[[66]](#cite_note-66)\n\nSupported by new industry and growing commerce, Bristol's population (66,000 in 1801), quintupled during the 19th century,[[67]](#cite_note-67) resulting in the creation of new suburbs such as [Clifton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clifton,_Bristol \"Clifton, Bristol\") and [Cotham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotham,_Bristol \"Cotham, Bristol\"). These provide architectural examples from the Georgian to the Regency style, with many fine terraces and villas facing the road, and at right angles to it. In the early 19th century, the romantic [medieval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval \"Medieval\") [gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_revival \"Gothic revival\") style appeared, partially as a reaction against the [symmetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symmetry \"Symmetry\") of [Palladianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palladianism \"Palladianism\"), and can be seen in buildings such as the [Bristol City Museum and Art Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_City_Museum_and_Art_Gallery \"Bristol City Museum and Art Gallery\"),[[68]](#cite_note-68) the [Royal West of England Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_West_of_England_Academy \"Royal West of England Academy\"),[[69]](#cite_note-69) and [The Victoria Rooms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Victoria_Rooms \"The Victoria Rooms\").[[70]](#cite_note-Victoria_Rooms-70) [Riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_riots \"Bristol riots\") broke out in 1793[[71]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHunt1818-71) and 1831; the first over the renewal of [tolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toll_road \"Toll road\") on Bristol Bridge, and the second against the rejection of the second [Reform Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Act_1832 \"Reform Act 1832\") by the [House of Lords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Lords \"House of Lords\").[[72]](#cite_note-72) The population by 1841 had reached 140,158.[[73]](#cite_note-73)\n\nThe Diocese of Bristol had undergone several boundary changes by 1897 when it was \"reconstituted\" into the configuration which has lasted into the 21st century.[[74]](#cite_note-74)\n\n### 20th century\n\n![An old ordnance survey map of Bristol, showing roads, railways, rivers and contours.](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Bristol_map_1946.jpg/250px-Bristol_map_1946.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nFrom a population of about 330,000 in 1901, Bristol grew steadily during the 20th century, peaking at 428,089 in 1971.[[75]](#cite_note-visiontime-75) Its Avonmouth docklands were enlarged during the early 1900s by the Royal Edward Dock.[[76]](#cite_note-76) Another new dock, the [Royal Portbury Dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Portbury_Dock \"Royal Portbury Dock\"), opened across the river from Avonmouth during the 1970s.[[77]](#cite_note-77) As air travel grew in the first half of the century, aircraft manufacturers built factories.[[78]](#cite_note-78) The unsuccessful [Bristol International Exhibition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_International_Exhibition \"Bristol International Exhibition\") was held on Ashton Meadows in the [Bower Ashton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bower_Ashton \"Bower Ashton\") area in 1914.[[79]](#cite_note-79) After the premature closure of the exhibition the site was used, until 1919, as [barracks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barracks \"Barracks\") for the [Gloucestershire Regiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gloucestershire_Regiment \"Gloucestershire Regiment\") during [World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_I \"World War I\").[[80]](#cite_note-80)[[81]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurlton201460–90-81)\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/St_Mary_le_Port_Church%2C_Bristol%2C_BRO_Picbox-3-Blitz-4a%2C_1250x1250.jpg/250px-St_Mary_le_Port_Church%2C_Bristol%2C_BRO_Picbox-3-Blitz-4a%2C_1250x1250.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\n[Bristol was heavily damaged by Luftwaffe raids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Blitz \"Bristol Blitz\") during [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II \"World War II\"); about 1,300 people living or working in the city were killed, and nearly 100,000 buildings were damaged, at least 3,000 beyond repair.[[82]](#cite_note-82)[[83]](#cite_note-83) The original central shopping area, near the bridge and castle, [is now a park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castle_Park,_Bristol \"Castle Park, Bristol\") containing two bombed churches and fragments of the castle. A third bomb-damaged church nearby, [St Nicholas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Nicholas_Church,_Bristol \"St Nicholas Church, Bristol\"), was restored and, after a period as a museum, has now re-opened as a church.[[84]](#cite_note-84) It houses a 1756 [William Hogarth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Hogarth \"William Hogarth\") [triptych](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triptych \"Triptych\") painted for the high altar of [St Mary Redcliffe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Mary_Redcliffe \"St Mary Redcliffe\"). The church also has statues of [King Edward I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Edward_I \"King Edward I\") (moved from [Arno's Court Triumphal Arch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arno's_Court_Triumphal_Arch \"Arno's Court Triumphal Arch\")) and [King Edward III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Edward_III \"King Edward III\") (taken from Lawfords' Gate in the city walls when they were demolished about 1760), and 13th-century statues of [Robert, 1st Earl of Gloucester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert,_1st_Earl_of_Gloucester \"Robert, 1st Earl of Gloucester\") (builder of [Bristol Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Castle \"Bristol Castle\"))[[85]](#cite_note-85) and [Geoffrey de Montbray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geoffrey_de_Montbray \"Geoffrey de Montbray\") (who built the city's walls) from Bristol's [Newgate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newgate,_Bristol \"Newgate, Bristol\").[[86]](#cite_note-86)\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Ambrose_Rd%2C_Bristol.jpg/250px-Ambrose_Rd%2C_Bristol.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nThe rebuilding of [Bristol city centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_city_centre \"Bristol city centre\") was characterised by 1960s and 1970s [skyscrapers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skyscraper \"Skyscraper\"), [mid-century modern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mid-century_modern \"Mid-century modern\") architecture and [road building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20th_Century_Road_Schemes_in_Bristol \"20th Century Road Schemes in Bristol\"). Beginning in the 1980s some [main roads were closed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20th_Century_Road_Schemes_in_Bristol#Queen_Square,_Redcliffe_Way_and_The_Centre \"20th Century Road Schemes in Bristol\"), the [Georgian-era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_era \"Georgian era\") [Queen Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Square,_Bristol \"Queen Square, Bristol\") and [Portland Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portland_Square,_Bristol \"Portland Square, Bristol\") were restored, the [Broadmead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadmead \"Broadmead\") shopping area regenerated, and one of the city centre's tallest mid-century towers was demolished.[[87]](#cite_note-87) Bristol's road infrastructure changed dramatically during the 1960s and 1970s with the development of the [M4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M4_motorway \"M4 motorway\") and [M5 motorways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M5_motorway \"M5 motorway\"), which meet at the [Almondsbury Interchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almondsbury_Interchange \"Almondsbury Interchange\") just north of the city and link Bristol with London (M4 eastbound), [Swansea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swansea \"Swansea\") (M4 westbound across the [Severn Estuary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Severn_Estuary \"Severn Estuary\")), [Exeter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exeter \"Exeter\") (M5 southbound) and [Birmingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham \"Birmingham\") (M5 northbound).[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed \"Wikipedia:Citation needed\")*] Bristol was bombed twice by the [IRA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisional_IRA \"Provisional IRA\"), in [1974](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_Bristol_bombing \"1974 Bristol bombing\") and again in [1978](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_terrorist_incidents_in_Great_Britain#1970s \"List of terrorist incidents in Great Britain\").[[88]](#cite_note-88)\n\nThe 20th-century relocation of the docks to [Avonmouth Docks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avonmouth_Docks \"Avonmouth Docks\") and [Royal Portbury Dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Portbury_Dock \"Royal Portbury Dock\"), 7 mi (11 km) downstream from the city centre, has allowed the redevelopment of the old dock area (the Floating Harbour).[[89]](#cite_note-89) Although the docks' existence was once in jeopardy (since the area was seen as a derelict industrial site), the inaugural 1996 [International Festival of the Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Festival_of_the_Sea,_1996 \"International Festival of the Sea, 1996\") held in and around the docks affirmed the area as a leisure asset of the city.[[90]](#cite_note-90)\n\n### 21st century\n\n![Bristol from Princes Wharf](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/72/Bristol_Waterfront.jpg/330px-Bristol_Waterfront.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nOn 7 June 2020 a [Statue of Edward Colston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statue_of_Edward_Colston \"Statue of Edward Colston\") was pulled down from its city centre plinth by protestors and pushed into the harbour.[[91]](#cite_note-91) The statue was recovered on 11 June and has become a museum exhibit.[[92]](#cite_note-92) The action followed more than a decade of debate over the statue and the wording of its plaque, which commemorated Colston's philanthropic work in the city while making no reference to his role in the [Royal African Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_African_Company \"Royal African Company\") and the [Bristol Slave Trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Slave_Trade \"Bristol Slave Trade\").[[93]](#cite_note-93)\n\n## Government\n\n![A large brick building, built in a shallow curve, with a central porch. In front of that is a pool and a water fountain.](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Bristol_Council_House_-_geograph.org.uk_-_197619.jpg/250px-Bristol_Council_House_-_geograph.org.uk_-_197619.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![ A coat of arms, with a shield showing a sailing ship and a castle with maned lions on either side, surmounted by the helmet from a suit of arms and two hands holding a snake and scales of justice. The motto at the bottom is \"Virtute et Industria\"](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Arms_of_Bristol_City_Council.svg/250px-Arms_of_Bristol_City_Council.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nBristol City council consists of 70 councillors representing 34 wards, with between one and three per ward serving four-year terms.[[94]](#cite_note-94) As of May 2024[[update]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Bristol&action=edit), no party has an overall controlling majority, but the [Green Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Party_of_England_and_Wales \"Green Party of England and Wales\") are the largest group on the council.\n\nPolitical makeup of the council[[95]](#cite_note-95)\n\n| Party | | Councillors |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n|  | [Green](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Party_of_England_and_Wales \"Green Party of England and Wales\") | 34 |\n|  | [Labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_Party_(UK) \"Labour Party (UK)\") | 19 |\n|  | [Liberal Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Democrats_(UK) \"Liberal Democrats (UK)\") | 9 |\n|  | [Conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK) \"Conservative Party (UK)\") | 7 |\n|  | [Independent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_politician \"Independent politician\") | 1 |\n|\n\nBetween 2012 and 2024, the council was led by a directly elected mayor, the [Mayor of Bristol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Bristol \"Mayor of Bristol\"). The post was abolished following a 2022 referendum, in which voters chose to replace it with a council committee system.[[96]](#cite_note-96)[[97]](#cite_note-97)\n\nThe [lord mayor of Bristol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mayors_of_Bristol \"List of mayors of Bristol\") is a figurehead elected each May by the city council. The lord mayor (2026) is Henry Michallat.[[3]](#cite_note-bbc_lord_mayor_2026-3)\n\nIn the [House of Commons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom \"House of Commons of the United Kingdom\"), Bristol is represented by five constituencies, [East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_East_(UK_Parliament_constituency) \"Bristol East (UK Parliament constituency)\"), [North East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_North_East_(UK_Parliament_constituency) \"Bristol North East (UK Parliament constituency)\"), [North West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_North_West_(UK_Parliament_constituency) \"Bristol North West (UK Parliament constituency)\"), [South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_South_(UK_Parliament_constituency) \"Bristol South (UK Parliament constituency)\"), and [Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Central_(UK_Parliament_constituency) \"Bristol Central (UK Parliament constituency)\").[[98]](#cite_note-98)\n\nThe city has a tradition of political activism. [Edmund Burke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_Burke \"Edmund Burke\"), MP for the [Bristol constituency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_(UK_Parliament_constituency) \"Bristol (UK Parliament constituency)\") for six years beginning in 1774, insisted that he was a Member of Parliament first and a representative of his constituents' interests second.[[99]](#cite_note-99)[[100]](#cite_note-100) Women's-rights advocate [Emmeline Pethick-Lawrence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmeline_Pethick-Lawrence \"Emmeline Pethick-Lawrence\") (1867–1954) was born in Bristol,[[101]](#cite_note-101) and the [left-winger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Left-wing_politics \"Left-wing politics\") [Tony Benn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Benn \"Tony Benn\") served as MP for [Bristol South East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_South_East \"Bristol South East\") from 1950 to 1960 and again from 1963 to 1983.[[102]](#cite_note-102) In 1963 the [Bristol Bus Boycott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Bus_Boycott \"Bristol Bus Boycott\"), following the [Bristol Omnibus Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Omnibus_Company \"Bristol Omnibus Company\")'s refusal to hire black drivers and conductors, drove the passage of the UK's 1965 [Race Relations Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_Relations_Act_1965 \"Race Relations Act 1965\").[[103]](#cite_note-103) The [1980 St Pauls riot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_St_Pauls_riot \"1980 St Pauls riot\") protested against racism and police harassment and showed mounting dissatisfaction with the socioeconomic circumstances of the city's Afro-Caribbean residents. Local support of [fair trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fair_trade \"Fair trade\") was recognised in 2005, when Bristol became a [fairtrade zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairtrade_zone \"Fairtrade zone\").[[104]](#cite_note-104)\n\nBristol is both a city and a county, since [King Edward III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Edward_III \"King Edward III\") granted it a county charter in 1373.[[22]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTELiddy200513-22) The county was expanded in 1835 to include suburbs such as [Clifton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clifton,_Bristol \"Clifton, Bristol\"), and it was named a [county borough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_borough \"County borough\") in 1889 when that designation was introduced.[[24]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayfield198517–23-24)\n\n![A tall church spire over a quayside with wooden sheds and boats covered with tarpaulins. In front of these on the water a twin masted sailing boat and a narrowboat](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/Bristol-St_Mary_Redcliffe-Docks.jpg/250px-Bristol-St_Mary_Redcliffe-Docks.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\n### Former county of Avon\n\nOn 1 April 1974, Bristol became a local government district of the administrative county of [Avon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avon_(county) \"Avon (county)\").[[105]](#cite_note-105) On 1 April 1996, Avon was abolished and Bristol became a [unitary authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_authority \"Unitary authority\").[[106]](#cite_note-106)\n\nThe former Avon area, called [Greater Bristol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Bristol \"Greater Bristol\") by the Government Office of the South West (now abolished) and others,[[107]](#cite_note-107) refers to the city and the three neighbouring local authorities‍—‌[Bath and North East Somerset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bath_and_North_East_Somerset \"Bath and North East Somerset\"), [North Somerset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Somerset \"North Somerset\") and [South Gloucestershire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Gloucestershire \"South Gloucestershire\") previously in Avon.\n\nThe [North Fringe of Bristol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Fringe_of_Bristol \"North Fringe of Bristol\"), a developed area between the Bristol city boundary and the M4, M5 and M32 motorways (now in South Gloucestershire) was so named as part of a 1987 [plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_plan \"Local plan\") prepared by the [Northavon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northavon \"Northavon\") District Council of Avon county.[[108]](#cite_note-108)\n\n### West of England Combined Authority\n\nThe [West of England Combined Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_of_England_Combined_Authority \"West of England Combined Authority\") was created on 9 February 2017.[[109]](#cite_note-SI_2017_No_126-109) Covering Bristol and the rest of the old Avon county with the exception of North Somerset, the new [combined authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combined_authority \"Combined authority\") has responsibility for regional planning, roads, and local transport, and to a lesser extent, education and business investment. The authority is led by the [Mayor of the West of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_the_West_of_England \"Mayor of the West of England\"), [Helen Godwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helen_Godwin \"Helen Godwin\").\n\n## Geography\n\n### Boundaries\n\n![Suspension bridge between two brick built towers, over a wooded gorge, showing mud and water at the bottom. In the distance are hills.](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/Clifton.bridge.arp.750pix.jpg/250px-Clifton.bridge.arp.750pix.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nBristol's boundaries can be defined in several ways, including those of the city itself, the developed area, or [Greater Bristol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Bristol \"Greater Bristol\").\n\nThe [city council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Bristol \"Politics of Bristol\") boundary is the narrowest definition of the city itself. However, it unusually includes a large, roughly rectangular section of the western [Severn Estuary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Severn_Estuary \"Severn Estuary\") ending at (but not including) the islands of [Flat Holm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flat_Holm \"Flat Holm\") (in [Cardiff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardiff \"Cardiff\"), Wales) and [Steep Holm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steep_Holm \"Steep Holm\").[[110]](#cite_note-110) This \"seaward extension\" can be traced back to the original boundary of the County of Bristol laid out in the charter[[111]](#cite_note-111) granted to the city by [Edward III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_III \"Edward III\") in 1373.[[112]](#cite_note-112)\n\nThe [Office for National Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_for_National_Statistics \"Office for National Statistics\") (ONS) has defined a [Bristol Urban Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Urban_Area \"Bristol Urban Area\"), which includes developed areas adjoining Bristol but outside the city-council boundary, such as [Kingswood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingswood,_South_Gloucestershire \"Kingswood, South Gloucestershire\"), [Mangotsfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangotsfield \"Mangotsfield\"), [Stoke Gifford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stoke_Gifford \"Stoke Gifford\"), [Winterbourne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winterbourne,_Gloucestershire \"Winterbourne, Gloucestershire\"), [Almondsbury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almondsbury \"Almondsbury\"), [Easton in Gordano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Easton_in_Gordano \"Easton in Gordano\"), [Whitchurch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitchurch,_Bristol \"Whitchurch, Bristol\") village, [Filton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filton \"Filton\"), [Patchway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patchway \"Patchway\") and [Bradley Stoke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bradley_Stoke \"Bradley Stoke\"), but excludes undeveloped areas within that boundary.[[113]](#cite_note-113)\n\n![Rocky side to a gorge with a platform in front of a cave halfway up. To the right is a road and a river. In the distance are a suspension bridge and buildings.](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/Avon_gorge_and_cave_arp.jpg/250px-Avon_gorge_and_cave_arp.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\n### Geography\n\nBristol lies within a [limestone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limestone \"Limestone\") area running from the [Mendip Hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mendip_Hills \"Mendip Hills\") in the south to the [Cotswolds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotswolds \"Cotswolds\") in the northeast.[[114]](#cite_note-114) The rivers Avon and Frome cut through the limestone to the underlying clay, creating Bristol's characteristically hilly landscape. The Avon flows from Bath in the east, through [flood plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flood_plains \"Flood plains\") and areas which were marshes before the city's growth. To the west, the Avon cuts through the limestone to form the Avon Gorge, formed largely by glacial meltwater after the [last ice age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quaternary_glaciation \"Quaternary glaciation\").[[115]](#cite_note-10.1144/GSL-115)\n\nThe gorge, which helped protect Bristol Harbour, has been quarried for stone to build the city, and its surrounding land has been protected from development as [The Downs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Downs,_Bristol \"The Downs, Bristol\") and Leigh Woods. The Avon [estuary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estuary \"Estuary\") and the gorge form the county boundary with [North Somerset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Somerset \"North Somerset\"), and the river flows into the [Severn Estuary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Severn_Estuary \"Severn Estuary\") at [Avonmouth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avonmouth \"Avonmouth\"). A smaller gorge, cut by the [Hazel Brook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazel_Brook \"Hazel Brook\") which flows into the [River Trym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Trym \"River Trym\"), crosses the [Blaise Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blaise_Castle \"Blaise Castle\") estate in northern Bristol.[[115]](#cite_note-10.1144/GSL-115)\n\nBristol is sometimes described, by its inhabitants, as being built on seven hills, like Rome. From 18th-century guidebooks, these seven hills were known as simply Bristol (the Old Town), Castle Hill, College Green, Kingsdown, St Michael's Hill, [Brandon Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brandon_Hill,_Bristol \"Brandon Hill, Bristol\") and Redcliffe Hill.[[116]](#cite_note-116) Other local hills include Red Lion Hill, [Barton Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barton_Hill,_Bristol \"Barton Hill, Bristol\"), [Lawrence Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_Hill,_Bristol \"Lawrence Hill, Bristol\"), Black Boy Hill, Constitution Hill, [Staple Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Staple_Hill,_Gloucestershire \"Staple Hill, Gloucestershire\"), [Windmill Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Windmill_Hill,_Bristol \"Windmill Hill, Bristol\"), Malborough Hill, Nine Tree Hill, Talbot, Brook Hill and Granby Hill.\n\nBristol is 106 mi (171 km) west of London, 77 mi (124 km) south-southwest of [Birmingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham \"Birmingham\") and 26 mi (42 km) east of the Welsh capital [Cardiff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardiff \"Cardiff\"). Areas adjoining the city fall within a loosely defined area known as [Greater Bristol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Bristol \"Greater Bristol\"). [Bath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bath,_Somerset \"Bath, Somerset\") is located 11 mi (18 km) southeast of the city centre, [Weston-super-Mare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weston-super-Mare \"Weston-super-Mare\") is 18 mi (29 km) to the southwest and the Welsh city of [Newport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newport,_Wales \"Newport, Wales\") is 19 mi (31 km) to the north west.\n\n### Climate\n\nThe climate is [oceanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_climate \"Oceanic climate\") ([Köppen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Köppen_climate_classification \"Köppen climate classification\"): *Cfb)*, milder than most places in England and United Kingdom.[[117]](#cite_note-117)[[118]](#cite_note-118) Located in southern England, Bristol is one of the warmest cities in the UK with a mean annual temperature of approximately 11 °C (52 °F).[[119]](#cite_note-119)[[120]](#cite_note-120) It is among the sunniest, with more than 1,600 hours of sunshine per year.[[121]](#cite_note-121) Although the city is partially sheltered by the Mendip Hills, it is exposed to the Severn Estuary and the [Bristol Channel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Channel \"Bristol Channel\"). Rain is fairly evenly distributed throughout the year, with autumn and winter the wetter seasons. The Atlantic Ocean influences Bristol's weather, keeping its average temperature above freezing throughout the year, but winter frosts are frequent and snow occasionally falls from early November to late April. Summers are warm and drier, with variable sunshine, rain and clouds, and spring weather is unsettled.[[122]](#cite_note-122)[[123]](#cite_note-123)\n\nThe weather stations nearest Bristol for which long-term climate data are available are Long Ashton (about 5 mi (8 km) south west of the city centre) and Bristol Weather Station, in the city centre. Data collection at these locations ended in 2002 and 2001, respectively, and following the closure of Filton Airfield, Almondsbury is the nearest weather station to the city.[[124]](#cite_note-MO_stations_2024-124) Temperatures at Long Ashton from 1959 to 2002 ranged from 33.5 °C (92.3 °F) in July 1976[[125]](#cite_note-125) to −14.4 °C (6.1 °F) in January 1982.[[126]](#cite_note-126) Monthly high temperatures since 2002 at Filton exceeding those recorded at Long Ashton include 25.7 °C (78.3 °F) in April 2003,[[127]](#cite_note-127) 34.5 °C (94.1 °F) in July 2006[[128]](#cite_note-128) and 26.8 °C (80.2 °F) in October 2011.[[129]](#cite_note-129) The lowest recent temperature at Filton was −10.1 °C (13.8 °F) in December 2010.[[130]](#cite_note-130) Although large cities in general experience an [urban heat island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_heat_island \"Urban heat island\") effect, with warmer temperatures than their surrounding rural areas, this phenomenon is minimal in Bristol.[[131]](#cite_note-131)\n\n| Climate data for [Filton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filton \"Filton\"),[[a]](#cite_note-132) elevation: 48 m (157 ft),
