Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Met_Office \"Met Office\")[[132]](#cite_note-134) | | | | | | | | | | | | | |\n| Source 2: [KNMI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Netherlands_Meteorological_Institute \"Royal Netherlands Meteorological Institute\")[[133]](#cite_note-135) | | | | | | | | | | | | | |\n\n### Environment\n\nBristol was ranked as Britain's most sustainable city (based on its environmental performance, quality of life, [future-proofing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Future-proofing \"Future-proofing\") and approaches to climate change, recycling and biodiversity), topping environmental charity [Forum for the Future](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forum_for_the_Future \"Forum for the Future\")'s 2008 [Sustainable Cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustainable_city \"Sustainable city\") Index.[[134]](#cite_note-136)[[135]](#cite_note-137) Local initiatives include [Sustrans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustrans \"Sustrans\") (creators of the [National Cycle Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Cycle_Network \"National Cycle Network\"), founded as Cyclebag in 1977)[[136]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTECottonGrimshaw2002-138) and Resourcesaver, a non-profit business established in 1988 by Avon Friends of the Earth.[[137]](#cite_note-139) The city regularly features in the annual *[Sunday Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunday_Times \"Sunday Times\")* lists of best places in Britain in which to live, topping the list of cities in 2014 and 2017.[[138]](#cite_note-140)[[139]](#cite_note-141) The city received the 2015 [European Green Capital Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Green_Capital_Award \"European Green Capital Award\"), becoming the first UK city to receive this award.[[140]](#cite_note-142)\n\nIn 2019 Bristol City Council voted in favour of banning all privately owned [diesel cars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diesel_engine \"Diesel engine\") from the city centre.[[141]](#cite_note-bbc._Bris-143) Since then, the plans have been revised in favour of a [clean air zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clean_air_zone \"Clean air zone\") whereby older and more polluting vehicles will be charged to drive through the city centre. The Clean Air Zone came into effect in November 2022.[[142]](#cite_note-144)\n\n### Green belt\n\nThe city has a green belt mainly along its southern fringes, taking in small areas within the [Ashton Court Estate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashton_Court \"Ashton Court\"), South Bristol crematorium and cemetery, High Ridge common and Whitchurch, with a further area around Frenchay Farm. The belt extends outside the city boundaries into surrounding counties and districts, for several miles in places, to afford protection from [urban sprawl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_sprawl \"Urban sprawl\") to surrounding villages and towns.\n\n## Demographics\n\n### Ethnicity\n\nIn the 2021 census, the ethnic composition of the city of Bristol comprised:[[143]](#cite_note-2021ethnicity-145)[[a]](#cite_note-146)\n\nEthnic groups in the city of Bristol\n\n| Ethnic group | 2001 census[[144]](#cite_note-147) | 2011 census[[145]](#cite_note-148) | 2021 census[[143]](#cite_note-2021ethnicity-145) |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| White | 91.8% | 84.0% | 81.1% |\n| Asian[[b]](#cite_note-149) | 3.4% | 5.5% | 6.6% |\n| Black | 2.3% | 6.0% | 5.9% |\n| Mixed | 2.1% | 3.6% | 4.5% |\n| Other | 0.3% | 0.9% | 1.9% |\n\n### Religion\n\nIn the 2021 census, the religious composition of the city of Bristol comprised: 32.2% Christian, 51.4% no religion, 6.7% Muslim, 0.8% Hindu, 0.6% Buddhist, 0.5% Sikh, 0.3% Jewish, 0.8% Other religion, and 6.9% not stated.\n\nReligion in the city of Bristol\n\n| Religion | 2001 census[[146]](#cite_note-150) | 2011 census[[147]](#cite_note-151) | 2021 census[[148]](#cite_note-152) |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| Christian | 62.1% | 46.8% | 32.2% |\n| No religion | 24.5% | 37.4% | 51.4% |\n| Muslim | 2.0% | 5.1% | 6.7% |\n| Hindu | 0.6% | 0.6% | 0.8% |\n| Buddhist | 0.4% | 0.6% | 0.6% |\n| Sikh | 0.5% | 0.5% | 0.5% |\n| Jewish | 0.2% | 0.2% | 0.3% |\n| Other religion | 0.5% | 0.7% | 0.8% |\n| Not stated | 9.3% | 8.1% | 6.9% |\n\nAmong the notable [Christian churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_churches_in_Bristol \"List of churches in Bristol\") are the [Anglican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican \"Anglican\") [Bristol Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Cathedral \"Bristol Cathedral\") and [St Mary Redcliffe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Mary_Redcliffe \"St Mary Redcliffe\") and the Roman Catholic [Clifton Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clifton_Cathedral \"Clifton Cathedral\"). [Nonconformist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonconformist_(Protestantism) \"Nonconformist (Protestantism)\") chapels include [Buckingham Baptist Chapel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buckingham_Baptist_Chapel \"Buckingham Baptist Chapel\") and [John Wesley's New Room](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Wesley's_New_Room \"John Wesley's New Room\") in Broadmead.[[149]](#cite_note-153) After [St James' Presbyterian Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_James'_Presbyterian_Church_of_England,_Bristol \"St James' Presbyterian Church of England, Bristol\") was [bombed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Blitz \"The Blitz\") on 24 November 1940, it was never again used as a church;[[150]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuncanWebb199086-154) although its [bell tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bell_tower \"Bell tower\") remains, its [nave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nave \"Nave\") was converted into offices.[[151]](#cite_note-155) The city has eleven mosques,[[152]](#cite_note-156) several Buddhist meditation centres,[[153]](#cite_note-157) a [Hindu temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_temple \"Hindu temple\"),[[154]](#cite_note-158) [Reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Movement_for_Reform_Judaism \"Movement for Reform Judaism\") and Orthodox-Jewish synagogues[[155]](#cite_note-159) and four [Sikh temples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_temples \"Sikh temples\").[[156]](#cite_note-160)[[157]](#cite_note-161)[[158]](#cite_note-162)\n\n### Bristol conurbation\n\nThe population of [Bristol's contiguous urban area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Urban_Area \"Bristol Urban Area\") was put at 551,066 by the ONS based on Census 2001 data.[[159]](#cite_note-ANameSoThatWeDontHaveToUseIbidForTheNextRef-163) In 2006 the ONS estimated Bristol's urban-area population at 587,400,[[160]](#cite_note-164) making it England's sixth-most populous city and tenth-most populous urban area.[[159]](#cite_note-ANameSoThatWeDontHaveToUseIbidForTheNextRef-163)\nAt 3,599/km2 (9,321/sq mi) it has the seventh-highest population density of any English district.[[161]](#cite_note-165) According to data from 2019, the urban area has the 11th-largest population in the UK, with a population of 670,000.[[162]](#cite_note-UrbanPopulation-166)\n\nIn 2007 the [European Spatial Planning Observation Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Spatial_Planning_Observation_Network \"European Spatial Planning Observation Network\") (ESPON) defined Bristol's [functional urban area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Functional_urban_area \"Functional urban area\") as including Weston-super-Mare, Bath and Clevedon with a total population of 1.04 million, the twelfth largest of the UK.[[163]](#cite_note-167)\n\n## Economy\n\n![Two ornate metal pillars with large dishes on top in a paved street, with an eighteenth-century stone building behind, upon which can be seen the words \"Tea Blenders Estabklishec 177-\". People sitting at café-style tables outside. On the right are iron railings.](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/42/BristolTheNails.jpg/250px-BristolTheNails.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nBristol has a long history of trade, originally exporting wool cloth and importing fish, wine, grain and dairy products;[[164]](#cite_note-168) later imports were tobacco, tropical fruits and plantation goods. Major imports are motor vehicles, grain, timber, [produce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Produce \"Produce\") and petroleum products.[[165]](#cite_note-169)\n\nThe city's economy also relies on the [aerospace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerospace \"Aerospace\"), defence, media, information technology, financial services and tourism industries.[[166]](#cite_note-170)[[167]](#cite_note-171) The [Ministry of Defence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Defence_(United_Kingdom) \"Ministry of Defence (United Kingdom)\") (MoD)'s Procurement Executive, later known as the [Defence Procurement Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defence_Procurement_Agency \"Defence Procurement Agency\") and [Defence Equipment and Support](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defence_Equipment_and_Support \"Defence Equipment and Support\"), moved to its headquarters to [Abbey Wood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MOD_Abbey_Wood \"MOD Abbey Wood\"), Filton, in 1995. This organisation, with a staff of 12,000 to 13,000, procures and supports MoD equipment.[[168]](#cite_note-172) One of the UK's most popular tourist destinations, Bristol was selected in 2009 as one of the world's top-ten cities by international travel publishers [Dorling Kindersley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dorling_Kindersley \"Dorling Kindersley\") in their *[Eyewitness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eyewitness_Books \"Eyewitness Books\")* guides for young adults.[[169]](#cite_note-173)\n\nBristol is one of the eight-largest regional English cities that make up the [Core Cities Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Core_Cities_Group \"Core Cities Group\"), and is ranked as a Gamma level [global city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_city \"Global city\") by the [Globalization and World Cities Research Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalization_and_World_Cities_Research_Network \"Globalization and World Cities Research Network\"), the fourth-highest-ranked English city.[[170]](#cite_note-174) In 2017 Bristol's [gross domestic product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_domestic_product \"Gross domestic product\") was £88.448 billion.[[171]](#cite_note-subregionalgva-175)[[172]](#cite_note-176) Its [per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Per_capita \"Per capita\") GDP was £46,000 ($65,106, €57,794), which was some 65% above the national average, the third-highest of any English city (after London and [Nottingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nottingham \"Nottingham\")) and the sixth-highest of any city in the United Kingdom (behind London, [Edinburgh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edinburgh \"Edinburgh\"), Glasgow, [Belfast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belfast \"Belfast\") and Nottingham).[[171]](#cite_note-subregionalgva-175) According to the 2011 census, Bristol's unemployment rate (claiming [Jobseeker's Allowance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jobseeker's_Allowance \"Jobseeker's Allowance\")) was three per cent, compared with two per cent for [South West England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_West_England \"South West England\") and the national average of four per cent.[[173]](#cite_note-177)\n\nAlthough Bristol's economy no longer relies upon its [port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Bristol \"Port of Bristol\"), which was moved to docks at Avonmouth during the 1870s[[174]](#cite_note-178) and to the Royal Portbury Dock in 1977 as ship size increased, it is the largest importer of cars to the UK. Until 1991, the port was publicly owned; it is leased, with £330 million invested and its annual tonnage increasing from 3.9 million long tons (4 million tonnes) to 11.8 million (12 million).[[175]](#cite_note-179) Tobacco importing and cigarette manufacturing have ceased, but the importation of wine and spirits continues.[[176]](#cite_note-180)\n\nThe financial services sector employs 59,000 in the city,[[177]](#cite_note-181) and 50 micro-electronics and silicon design companies employ about 5,000. In 1983, [Hewlett-Packard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hewlett-Packard \"Hewlett-Packard\") opened its national research laboratory in Bristol.[[178]](#cite_note-182)[[179]](#cite_note-183) In 2014 the city was ranked seventh in the \"top 10 UK destinations\" by [TripAdvisor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TripAdvisor \"TripAdvisor\").[[180]](#cite_note-184)\n\nDuring the 20th century, Bristol's manufacturing activities expanded to include aircraft production at Filton by the [Bristol Aeroplane Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Aeroplane_Company \"Bristol Aeroplane Company\") and aircraft-engine manufacturing by Bristol Aero Engines (later [Rolls-Royce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rolls-Royce_Holdings \"Rolls-Royce Holdings\")) at Patchway. Bristol Aeroplane was known for their World War I [Bristol Fighter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_F.2_Fighter \"Bristol F.2 Fighter\")[[181]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoyne2002105-185) and World War II [Blenheim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Blenheim \"Bristol Blenheim\") and [Beaufighter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaufighter \"Beaufighter\") planes.[[181]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoyne2002105-185) During the 1950s, they were a major English manufacturer of civilian aircraft, known for the [Freighter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Freighter \"Bristol Freighter\"), [Britannia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Britannia \"Bristol Britannia\") and [Brabazon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Brabazon \"Bristol Brabazon\"). The company diversified into automobile manufacturing during the 1940s, producing hand-built [luxury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luxury_vehicle \"Luxury vehicle\") [Bristol Cars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Cars \"Bristol Cars\") at their factory in Filton, and the Bristol Cars company was spun off in 1960.[[182]](#cite_note-186) The city also gave its name to Bristol buses, which were manufactured in the city from 1908 to 1983: by Bristol Tramways until 1955, and from 1955 to 1983 by [Bristol Commercial Vehicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Commercial_Vehicles \"Bristol Commercial Vehicles\").[[183]](#cite_note-187)\n\n![A view from below of an aeroplane in flight, with a slender fuselage and swept back wings.](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/06/Concorde_on_Bristol.jpg/250px-Concorde_on_Bristol.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nFilton played a key role in the Anglo-French [Concorde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concorde \"Concorde\") supersonic airliner project during the 1960s. The British Concorde prototype made its maiden flight from Filton to [RAF Fairford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RAF_Fairford \"RAF Fairford\") on 9 April 1969, five weeks after the French test flight.[[184]](#cite_note-188) In 2003 [British Airways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Airways \"British Airways\") and [Air France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_France \"Air France\") decided to discontinue Concorde flights, retiring the aircraft to locations (primarily museums) worldwide. On 26 November 2003 Concorde 216 made the final Concorde flight, returning to [Bristol Filton Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Filton_Airport \"Bristol Filton Airport\") as the centrepiece of a proposed air museum which is planned to include the existing Bristol Aero collection (including a Bristol Britannia).[[185]](#cite_note-189)\n\nThe aerospace industry remains a major sector of the local economy.[[186]](#cite_note-190) Major aerospace companies in Bristol include [BAE Systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BAE_Systems \"BAE Systems\"), a merger of [Marconi Electronic Systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marconi_Electronic_Systems \"Marconi Electronic Systems\") and [BAe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BAe \"BAe\") (the latter a merger of BAC, Hawker Siddeley and Scottish Aviation). [Airbus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airbus \"Airbus\")[[187]](#cite_note-191) and Rolls-Royce are also based at Filton, and aerospace engineering is an area of research at the University of the West of England. Another aviation company in the city is [Cameron Balloons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameron_Balloons \"Cameron Balloons\"), who manufacture [hot air balloons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot_air_balloon \"Hot air balloon\");[[188]](#cite_note-192) each August the city hosts the [Bristol International Balloon Fiesta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_International_Balloon_Fiesta \"Bristol International Balloon Fiesta\"), one of Europe's largest hot-air balloon festivals.[[189]](#cite_note-193)\n\nIn 2005, Bristol was named by the UK government one of England's six science cities.[[190]](#cite_note-194)[[191]](#cite_note-195) A £500 million shopping centre, [Cabot Circus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabot_Circus \"Cabot Circus\"), opened in 2008 amidst predictions by developers and politicians that the city would become one of England's top ten retail destinations.[[192]](#cite_note-196) The [Bristol Temple Quarter Enterprise Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Temple_Quarter_Enterprise_Zone \"Bristol Temple Quarter Enterprise Zone\"), focused on creative, high-tech and low-carbon industries around [Bristol Temple Meads railway station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Temple_Meads_railway_station \"Bristol Temple Meads railway station\"),[[193]](#cite_note-BP1-197) was announced in 2011[[194]](#cite_note-198) and launched the following year.[[193]](#cite_note-BP1-197) The 70 ha (170-acre) [Urban Enterprise Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_Enterprise_Zone \"Urban Enterprise Zone\") has streamlined [planning procedures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planning_permission \"Planning permission\") and reduced [business rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_rates_in_England_and_Wales \"Business rates in England and Wales\"). Rates generated by the zone are channelled to five other designated enterprise areas in the region:[[195]](#cite_note-199) Avonmouth, Bath, [Bristol and Bath Science Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_and_Bath_Science_Park \"Bristol and Bath Science Park\") in Emersons Green, Filton, and Weston-super-Mare. Bristol is the only big city whose wealth per capita is higher than that of Britain as a whole. The wider region has one of the biggest aerospace hubs in the UK, centred on Airbus, Rolls-Royce and GKN at Filton airfield.[[196]](#cite_note-200)\n\nBetween 2012 and 2020, the city had the largest circulating [community currency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_currency \"Community currency\") in the UK, the [Bristol Pound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Pound \"Bristol Pound\"), which was [pegged](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fixed_exchange_rate_system \"Fixed exchange rate system\") to the [pound sterling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pound_sterling \"Pound sterling\") before it ceased operation.\n\n![A panoramic view looking over a cityscape of office blocks, old buildings, church spires and a multi-story car park. In the distance are hills.](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/Panorama_of_Bristol.jpg/1920px-Panorama_of_Bristol.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\n## Culture\n\n### Arts\n\n![An imposing eighteenth-century building with three entrance archways, large first-floor windows and an ornate peaked gable end above.](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Coopers%27_Hall_front.jpg/250px-Coopers%27_Hall_front.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![A long two-storey building with 4 cranes in front on the quayside. Two tugboats are moored at the quay.](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/34/Bristol_pw_from_ms.jpg/250px-Bristol_pw_from_ms.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nBristol has a thriving arts scene. Some of the modern venues and modern digital production companies have merged with legacy production companies based in old buildings around the city. In 2008 the city was a finalist for the 2008 [European Capital of Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Capital_of_Culture \"European Capital of Culture\"), although the title was awarded to Liverpool.[[197]](#cite_note-201) The city was designated \"City of Film\" by [UNESCO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO \"UNESCO\") in 2017 and has been a member of the [Creative Cities Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creative_Cities_Network \"Creative Cities Network\") since then.[[198]](#cite_note-202)\n\nThe [Bristol Old Vic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Old_Vic \"Bristol Old Vic\"), founded in 1946 as an offshoot of [The Old Vic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Old_Vic \"The Old Vic\") in London, occupies the 1766 Theatre Royal (607 seats) on [King Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Street,_Bristol \"King Street, Bristol\") and the Weston Studio in the adjacent Coopers' Hall (built in 1743), with a 2018 bar and foyer area. The Theatre Royal, a grade I [listed building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Listed_building \"Listed building\"),[[199]](#cite_note-203)[[200]](#cite_note-gradei-204) is the oldest continuously operating theatre in England.[[201]](#cite_note-205) The [Bristol Old Vic Theatre School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Old_Vic_Theatre_School \"Bristol Old Vic Theatre School\") (which originated in King Street) is a separate company, and the [Bristol Hippodrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Hippodrome \"Bristol Hippodrome\") is a 1,951-seat theatre for national touring productions. Other smaller theatres include the [Tobacco Factory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tobacco_Factory \"Tobacco Factory\"), [QEH](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Elizabeth's_Hospital \"Queen Elizabeth's Hospital\"), the Redgrave Theatre at [Clifton College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clifton_College \"Clifton College\"), The Wardrobe Theatre, Bristol Improv Theatre, and the Alma Theatre. Bristol's theatre scene features several companies as well as the Old Vic, including [Show of Strength](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Show_of_Strength_Theatre_Company \"Show of Strength Theatre Company\"), [Shakespeare at the Tobacco Factory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shakespeare_at_the_Tobacco_Factory \"Shakespeare at the Tobacco Factory\") and Travelling Light. Theatre Bristol is a partnership between the city council, [Arts Council England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arts_Council_England \"Arts Council England\") and local residents to develop the city's theatre industry.[[202]](#cite_note-206) Several organisations support Bristol theatre; the Residence (an artist-led community) provides office, social and rehearsal space for theatre and performance companies,[[203]](#cite_note-207) and [Equity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equity_(trade_union) \"Equity (trade union)\") has a branch in the city.[[204]](#cite_note-208)\n\n![A painting on a building showing a naked man hanging by one hand from a window sill. A man in a suit looks out of the window, shading his eyes with his right hand, behind him stands a woman in her underwear.](//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/3/3f/Banksy-ps2.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)\n\nThe city has many venues for live music, its largest being the 2,000-seat [Bristol Beacon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Beacon \"Bristol Beacon\"), previously Colston Hall, named after [Edward Colston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Colston \"Edward Colston\"). Others include the [Bristol Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O2_Academy_Bristol \"O2 Academy Bristol\"), [The Fleece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wool_Hall,_Bristol \"Wool Hall, Bristol\"), [The Croft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Croft_(music_venue)?action=edit&redlink=1 \"The Croft (music venue) (page does not exist)\"), the Exchange, Fiddlers, the [Victoria Rooms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victoria_Rooms,_Bristol \"Victoria Rooms, Bristol\"), Rough Trade, [Trinity Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity_Centre \"Trinity Centre\"), [St George's Bristol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_George's_Bristol \"St George's Bristol\") and several pubs, from the jazz-oriented [The Old Duke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Old_Duke,_Bristol \"The Old Duke, Bristol\") to rock at the Fleece and [indie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_music \"Independent music\") bands at the Louisiana.[[205]](#cite_note-209)[[206]](#cite_note-210) In 2010 [PRS for Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PRS_for_Music \"PRS for Music\") called Bristol the UK's most musical city, based on the number of its members born there relative to the city's population.[[207]](#cite_note-211)\n\nSince the late 1970s, Bristol has been home to bands combining [punk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punk_rock \"Punk rock\"), [funk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funk_rock \"Funk rock\"), [dub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dub_music \"Dub music\") and [political consciousness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_consciousness \"Political consciousness\"). With [trip hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trip_hop \"Trip hop\") and [Bristol Sound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Sound \"Bristol Sound\") artists such as [Tricky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tricky_(musician) \"Tricky (musician)\"),[[208]](#cite_note-212) [Portishead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portishead_(band) \"Portishead (band)\")[[209]](#cite_note-213) and [Massive Attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massive_Attack \"Massive Attack\"),[[210]](#cite_note-214) the [list of bands from Bristol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_bands_from_Bristol \"List of bands from Bristol\") is extensive. The city is a stronghold of [drum and bass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drum_and_bass \"Drum and bass\"), with artists such as [Roni Size](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roni_Size \"Roni Size\")'s [Mercury Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercury_Prize \"Mercury Prize\")-winning [Reprazent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reprazent \"Reprazent\"),[[211]](#cite_note-215) as [DJ Krust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DJ_Krust \"DJ Krust\"),[[212]](#cite_note-216) [More Rockers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/More_Rockers \"More Rockers\")[[213]](#cite_note-217) and [TC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TC_(musician) \"TC (musician)\").[[214]](#cite_note-218) Musicians were at the centre of the broader Bristol urban-culture scene which received international media attention during the 1990s.[[215]](#cite_note-219) [The Downs Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Downs_Festival \"The Downs Festival\") is also a yearly occurrence where both local and well-known bands play. Since its inception in 2016, it has become a major event in the city.\n\nThe [Bristol Museum and Art Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Museum_and_Art_Gallery \"Bristol Museum and Art Gallery\") houses a collection encompassing [natural history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_history \"Natural history\"), archaeology, local glassware, [Chinese ceramics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_ceramics \"Chinese ceramics\") and art. The [M Shed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M_Shed \"M Shed\") museum opened in 2011 on the site of the former [Bristol Industrial Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Industrial_Museum \"Bristol Industrial Museum\").[[216]](#cite_note-220) Both are operated by Bristol Culture and Creative Industries, which also runs three historic houses‍—‌the [Tudor Red Lodge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Lodge_Museum,_Bristol \"Red Lodge Museum, Bristol\"), the [Georgian House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_House,_Bristol \"Georgian House, Bristol\") and [Blaise Castle House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blaise_Castle_House \"Blaise Castle House\"); and [Bristol Archives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Archives \"Bristol Archives\").[[217]](#cite_note-221) The 18th- and 19th-century portrait painter [Thomas Lawrence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Lawrence \"Thomas Lawrence\"), 19th-century architect [Francis Greenway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Greenway \"Francis Greenway\") (designer of many of Sydney's first buildings) were born in the city. The [graffiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graffiti \"Graffiti\") artist [Banksy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banksy \"Banksy\") is believed to be from Bristol, and many of his works are on display in the city.\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4b/Wallace%26GromitModels2019.jpg/250px-Wallace%26GromitModels2019.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nThe [Watershed Media Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watershed_Media_Centre \"Watershed Media Centre\") and [Arnolfini gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnolfini_gallery \"Arnolfini gallery\") (both in dockside warehouses) exhibit contemporary art, photography and cinema, and the city's oldest gallery is at the [Royal West of England Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_West_of_England_Academy \"Royal West of England Academy\") in Clifton.[[218]](#cite_note-222) The nomadic [Antlers Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antlers_Gallery \"Antlers Gallery\") opened in 2010, moving into empty spaces on [Park Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Park_Street,_Bristol \"Park Street, Bristol\"), on [Whiteladies Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whiteladies_Road \"Whiteladies Road\") and in the Purifier House on Bristol's Harbourside.[[219]](#cite_note-223) [Stop-motion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stop-motion \"Stop-motion\") animation films and commercials (produced by [Aardman Animations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aardman_Animations \"Aardman Animations\")) are made in Bristol, such as *[Wallace & Gromit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallace_&_Gromit \"Wallace & Gromit\")* and *[Chicken Run](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicken_Run \"Chicken Run\")*, while Aardman has also branched out into computer-animation, such as *[Arthur Christmas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Christmas \"Arthur Christmas\")*.[[220]](#cite_note-224)[[221]](#cite_note-225) [Robert Newton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Newton \"Robert Newton\"), [Bobby Driscoll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobby_Driscoll \"Bobby Driscoll\") and other cast members of the 1950 [Walt Disney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney \"Walt Disney\") film *[Treasure Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treasure_Island_(1950_film) \"Treasure Island (1950 film)\")* (some scenes were filmed along the [harbourside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Harbour \"Bristol Harbour\")) visited the city along with Disney himself. Bristol is home to the [regional headquarters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadcasting_House,_Bristol \"Broadcasting House, Bristol\") of [BBC West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_West \"BBC West\") and the [BBC Natural History Unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_Natural_History_Unit \"BBC Natural History Unit\").[[222]](#cite_note-226) Locations in and around Bristol have featured in the BBC's natural-history programmes, including *[Animal Magic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_Magic_(TV_series) \"Animal Magic (TV series)\")* (filmed at [Bristol Zoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Zoo \"Bristol Zoo\")).[[223]](#cite_note-227)\n\nBristol is the birthplace of 18th-century poets [Robert Southey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Southey \"Robert Southey\")[[224]](#cite_note-228) and [Thomas Chatterton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Chatterton \"Thomas Chatterton\").[[225]](#cite_note-229) Southey (born on [Wine Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wine_Street,_Bristol \"Wine Street, Bristol\") in 1774) and his friend, [Samuel Taylor Coleridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Taylor_Coleridge \"Samuel Taylor Coleridge\"), married the Fricker sisters from the city.[[226]](#cite_note-230) [William Wordsworth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Wordsworth \"William Wordsworth\") spent time in Bristol,[[227]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewlyn20017-231) where [Joseph Cottle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Cottle \"Joseph Cottle\") published *[Lyrical Ballads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyrical_Ballads \"Lyrical Ballads\")* in 1798. Actor [Cary Grant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cary_Grant \"Cary Grant\") was born in Bristol, and comedians from the city include [Justin Lee Collins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justin_Lee_Collins \"Justin Lee Collins\"),[[228]](#cite_note-232) [Lee Evans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Evans_(comedian) \"Lee Evans (comedian)\"),[[229]](#cite_note-233) [Russell Howard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russell_Howard \"Russell Howard\")[[230]](#cite_note-234) and writer-comedian [Stephen Merchant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Merchant \"Stephen Merchant\").[[231]](#cite_note-235)\n\nThe author [John Betjeman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Betjeman \"John Betjeman\") wrote a poem called \"Bristol\".[[232]](#cite_note-JB_1-236) It begins:\n\nGreen upon the flooded Avon shone the after-storm-wet-sky,  \nQuick the struggling withy branches let the leaves of autumn fly,  \nAnd a star shone over Bristol, wonderfully far and high.\n\n— John Betjeman, Bristol\n\n### Architecture\n\n![Large, square two-storey house at the end of a dirt path](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/Kings_Weston_House%2C_Bristol._The_Garden_Front._Sir_John_Vanbrugh%2C_1712.jpg/250px-Kings_Weston_House%2C_Bristol._The_Garden_Front._Sir_John_Vanbrugh%2C_1712.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![A seventeenth-century timber-framed building with three gables and a traditional inn sign showing a picture of a sailing barge. Some drinkers sit at benches outside on a cobbled street. Other old buildings are further down the street, and in the background, part of a modern office building can be seen.](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Llandoger.trow.overall.arp.jpg/250px-Llandoger.trow.overall.arp.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nBristol has 51 [Grade I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grade_I_listed_buildings_in_Bristol \"Grade I listed buildings in Bristol\"),[[200]](#cite_note-gradei-204) 500 [Grade II\\*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grade_II*_listed_buildings_in_Bristol \"Grade II* listed buildings in Bristol\") and over 3,800 [Grade II listed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grade_II_listed_buildings_in_Bristol \"Grade II listed buildings in Bristol\") [buildings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Categories_of_listed_building \"Categories of listed building\")[[233]](#cite_note-237) in a variety of [architectural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architectural \"Architectural\") styles, from [medieval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_architecture \"Medieval architecture\") to modern. During the mid-19th century, [Bristol Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Byzantine \"Bristol Byzantine\"), a style unique to the city, was developed, and several examples have survived. Buildings from most [architectural periods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architectural_periods \"Architectural periods\") of the United Kingdom can be seen in the city. Surviving elements of the fortifications and castle date to the medieval period,[[234]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurrough19703-238) and the Church of St James dates back to the 12th century.[[235]](#cite_note-239)\n\nThe oldest Grade I listed buildings in Bristol are religious. [St James' Priory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_James'_Priory,_Bristol \"St James' Priory, Bristol\") was founded in 1129 as a [Benedictine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benedictine \"Benedictine\") priory by Earl [Robert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert,_1st_Earl_of_Gloucester \"Robert, 1st Earl of Gloucester\") of Gloucester, the illegitimate son of [Henry I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_I_of_England \"Henry I of England\").[[236]](#cite_note-240) The second-oldest is [Bristol Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Cathedral \"Bristol Cathedral\") and its associated [Great Gatehouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Great_Gatehouse \"The Great Gatehouse\").[[237]](#cite_note-241) Founded in 1140 as an [Augustinian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augustinians \"Augustinians\") monastery, the church became the seat of the [bishop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bishop \"Bishop\") and cathedral of the new Diocese of Bristol in 1542. Most of the medieval stonework, particularly the Elder Lady Chapel, is made from limestone taken from quarries around [Dundry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dundry \"Dundry\") and [Felton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felton,_Somerset \"Felton, Somerset\"), with [Bath stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bath_stone \"Bath stone\") being used in other areas.[[238]](#cite_note-242) Amongst the other churches included in the list is the 12th-century [St Mary Redcliffe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Mary_Redcliffe \"St Mary Redcliffe\") which is the second tallest building in Bristol. The church was described by [Queen Elizabeth I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Elizabeth_I \"Queen Elizabeth I\") as \"the fairest, goodliest, and most famous parish church in England.\"[[239]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurrough197013–14-243)\n\nSecular buildings include [The Red Lodge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Lodge_Museum,_Bristol \"Red Lodge Museum, Bristol\"), built in 1580 for John Yonge as a [lodge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gatehouse \"Gatehouse\") for a larger house that once stood on the site of the present [Bristol Beacon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Beacon \"Bristol Beacon\"), previously known as Colston Hall. It was added to in [Georgian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_architecture \"Georgian architecture\") times and restored in the early 20th century.[[240]](#cite_note-244) [St Bartholomew's Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Bartholomew's_Hospital,_Bristol \"St Bartholomew's Hospital, Bristol\") is a 12th-century town house which was incorporated into a monastery hospital. It was founded in 1240 by [Sir John la Warr, 2nd Baron De La Warr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earl_De_La_Warr \"Earl De La Warr\") (c. 1277–1347), and became [Bristol Grammar School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Grammar_School \"Bristol Grammar School\") from 1532 to 1767, and then [Queen Elizabeth's Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Elizabeth's_Hospital \"Queen Elizabeth's Hospital\") 1767–1847. The round piers predate the hospital, and may come from an aisled hall, the earliest remains of domestic architecture in the city, which was then adapted to form the hospital chapel.[[241]](#cite_note-245) Three 17th-century town houses which were attached to the hospital were incorporated into model workers' flats in 1865, and converted to offices in 1978. [St Nicholas's Almshouses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Nicholas's_Almshouses \"St Nicholas's Almshouses\") were built in 1652[[242]](#cite_note-246) to provide care for the poor. Several public houses were also built in this period, including the [Llandoger Trow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Llandoger_Trow \"Llandoger Trow\")[[243]](#cite_note-247) on King Street and the [Hatchet Inn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hatchet_Inn,_Bristol \"Hatchet Inn, Bristol\").[[244]](#cite_note-248)\n\n[Manor houses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manor_house \"Manor house\") include [Goldney Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goldney_Hall \"Goldney Hall\"), where the highly decorated [Grotto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grotto_at_Goldney_House \"Grotto at Goldney House\") dates from 1739.[[245]](#cite_note-249) Commercial buildings such as the Exchange[[246]](#cite_note-250) and [Old Post Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Post_Office,_Bristol \"Old Post Office, Bristol\")[[247]](#cite_note-251) from the 1740s are also included in the list. Residential buildings include the Georgian Portland Square[[248]](#cite_note-252) and the complex of small cottages around a green at [Blaise Hamlet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blaise_Hamlet \"Blaise Hamlet\"), which was built around 1811 for retired employees of [Quaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quaker \"Quaker\") [banker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banker \"Banker\") and [philanthropist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philanthropist \"Philanthropist\") [John Scandrett Harford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Scandrett_Harford \"John Scandrett Harford\"), who owned [Blaise Castle House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blaise_Castle_House \"Blaise Castle House\").[[249]](#cite_note-253)\n\nThe 18th-century [Kings Weston House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kings_Weston_House \"Kings Weston House\"), in northern Bristol, was designed by [John Vanbrugh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Vanbrugh \"John Vanbrugh\") and is the only Vanbrugh building in any UK city outside London. [Almshouses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almshouse \"Almshouse\")[[250]](#cite_note-254) and pubs from the same period[[251]](#cite_note-255) intermingle with modern development. Several Georgian [squares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garden_square \"Garden square\") were designed for the middle class as prosperity increased during the 18th century.[[252]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFoyle200419–21-256)\n\nDuring World War II, the city centre was heavily bombed during the [Bristol Blitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Blitz \"Bristol Blitz\").[[253]](#cite_note-257) The central shopping area near [Wine Street and Castle Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castle_Park,_Bristol \"Castle Park, Bristol\") was particularly hard-hit, and [the Dutch House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Dutch_House,_Bristol \"The Dutch House, Bristol\") and [St Peter's Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Peter's_Hospital,_Bristol \"St Peter's Hospital, Bristol\") were destroyed. Nevertheless, in 1961 [John Betjeman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Betjeman \"John Betjeman\") called Bristol \"the most beautiful, interesting and distinguished city in England\".[[254]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWinstone1985124-258)\n\n### Dialect\n\n![An ornate brick tower surrounded by trees. The tower has balconies and is surmounted by a pitched roof with an ornate figure at the apex.](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c4/Cabot_Tower_%28600px%29.jpg/250px-Cabot_Tower_%28600px%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nA dialect of English ([West Country English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Country_English \"West Country English\")), known as Bristolian, is spoken by longtime residents, who are known as Bristolians.[[255]](#cite_note-259) Bristol natives have a [rhotic accent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhotic_accent \"Rhotic accent\"), in which the post-vocalic *r* in *car* and *card* is pronounced (unlike in [Received Pronunciation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Received_Pronunciation \"Received Pronunciation\")). The city is regarded as one of the last locations in England, along with [Blackburn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackburn \"Blackburn\"), to preserve the traditional English rhotic R sound.[[256]](#cite_note-260) The unique feature of this accent is the 'Bristol (or terminal) l', in which *l* is appended to words ending in *a* or *o*.[[257]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHughes201286–88-261) Whether this is a broad *l* or a *w* is a subject of debate,[[258]](#cite_note-262) with *area* pronounced 'areal' or 'areaw'. The ending of *Bristol* is another example of the Bristol *l*. Bristolians pronounce *-a* and *-o* at the end of a word as *-aw* (*cinemaw*). To non-natives, the pronunciation suggests an *l* after the vowel.[[259]](#cite_note-263)[[260]](#cite_note-264)\n\nBristolian is characterised by use of *cassn't* instead of *can't* and *canst* and *dissn't* instead of *didst* and *didn't*.[[261]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlack1996172-265) The second-person singular was retained and children were admonished with \"Thee and thou, the Welshman's cow\". In Bristolian, as in French and German, the second-person singular was not used when speaking to a superior (except by the egalitarian [Quakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quakers \"Quakers\")). The pronoun *thee* is also used in the subject position (\"What bist thee doing?\"), and *I* or *he* in the object position (\"Give he to I.\").[[262]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrohmeyer200913-266) Linguist [Stanley Ellis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanley_Ellis_(linguist) \"Stanley Ellis (linguist)\"), who found that many dialect words in the Filton area were linked to aerospace work, described Bristolian as \"a cranky, crazy, crab-apple tree of language and with the sharpest, juiciest flavour that I've heard for a long time\".[[263]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEElmes200539-267)\n\n## Sport\n\n![In the foreground, twentieth-century housing can be seen amidst trees, and on the right, a tower block of flats. In the middle distance, a complex of red coloured buildings can be seen, and behind that, a steep-sided gorge with a suspension bridge spanning it. Eighteenth-century terraces on the right side of the gorge, the slopes of which are heavily wooded, and a tower can be seen in the distance on the skyline.](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/Ashton_Gate_%26_Bridge.jpg/250px-Ashton_Gate_%26_Bridge.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nBristol is represented by professional teams in all the major national sports. [Bristol City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_City \"Bristol City\") and [Bristol Rovers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Rovers \"Bristol Rovers\") are the city's main [football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_football \"Association football\") clubs. [Bristol Bears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Bears \"Bristol Bears\") ([rugby union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_union \"Rugby union\")) and [Gloucestershire County Cricket Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gloucestershire_County_Cricket_Club \"Gloucestershire County Cricket Club\") are also based in the city.\n\nThe two [Football League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_League \"Football League\") clubs are Bristol City and Bristol Rovers‍—‌the former being the only club from the city to play in the precursor to the [Premier League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premier_League \"Premier League\"). Bristol City, formed in 1894, were Division One runners-up in 1907 and lost the FA Cup final in 1909. In the First Division in 1976, they then sank to the bottom professional tier before reforming after a 1982 bankruptcy. They have been above Bristol Rovers in the Football League since 2000, and were promoted to the second tier of English football in 2007.[[264]](#cite_note-268) [Bristol City Women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_City_Women \"Bristol City Women\") were formerly based at [Twerton Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twerton_Park \"Twerton Park\"), but now share [Ashton Gate Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashton_Gate_Stadium \"Ashton Gate Stadium\") as a home venue with the men's team.[[265]](#cite_note-269)\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/Uplands_StandBRFC.JPG/250px-Uplands_StandBRFC.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nBristol Rovers, the oldest professional football team in the city, were formed in 1883 and promoted back into the Football League in 2015. They were third-tier champions twice ([Division Three South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Division_Three_South \"Division Three South\") in 1952–53 and [Division Three](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Division_Three \"Division Three\") in 1989–90), [Watney Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watney_Cup \"Watney Cup\") Winners (1972) and runners-up for the [Johnstone's Paint Trophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnstone's_Paint_Trophy \"Johnstone's Paint Trophy\") (2006–07), although they have never played in England's top division.\n\n[Bristol Bears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Bears \"Bristol Bears\") was formed in 1888 and has often competed at the highest level of rugby union.[[266]](#cite_note-270) The club played at the [Memorial Ground](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorial_Ground \"Memorial Ground\"), which it shared with Bristol Rovers from 1996, until moving to Ashton Gate Stadium in 2014.[[267]](#cite_note-271)[[268]](#cite_note-272) They changed their name from Bristol Rugby to Bristol Bears to coincide with their return to [Premiership Rugby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premiership_Rugby \"Premiership Rugby\") in [the 2018–19 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018-19_English_Premiership \"2018-19 English Premiership\").\n\nThe [first-class cricket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First-class_cricket \"First-class cricket\") club [Gloucestershire County Cricket Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gloucestershire_County_Cricket_Club \"Gloucestershire County Cricket Club\")[[269]](#cite_note-273) has its headquarters and plays the majority of its home games at the [Bristol County Ground](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_County_Ground \"Bristol County Ground\"), the only major international sports venue in the south-west of England. It was formed by the family of [W. G. Grace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W._G._Grace \"W. G. Grace\").[[270]](#cite_note-274)\n\n![A large number of hot air balloons taking off from a field which is surrounded by tents and stalls. The sun is low in the sky, and balloons can be seen flying into the distance.](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Bristol_International_Balloon_Fiesta.JPG/250px-Bristol_International_Balloon_Fiesta.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nBristol sponsors the annual [Bristol Half Marathon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Half_Marathon \"Bristol Half Marathon\") and hosted the [2001 IAAF World Half Marathon Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_IAAF_World_Half_Marathon_Championships \"2001 IAAF World Half Marathon Championships\").[[271]](#cite_note-275) Bristol has staged finishes and starts of the [Tour of Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tour_of_Britain \"Tour of Britain\") cycle race,[[272]](#cite_note-276) and facilities in the city were used as training camps for the [2012 London Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_London_Olympics \"2012 London Olympics\").[[273]](#cite_note-277) The [Bristol International Balloon Fiesta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_International_Balloon_Fiesta \"Bristol International Balloon Fiesta\"), a major UK hot-air ballooning event, is held each summer at Ashton Court.[[274]](#cite_note-278)\n\nBristol is the birthplace of the 2025 [Formula 1 World Champion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Formula_One_World_Drivers'_Champions \"List of Formula One World Drivers' Champions\") [Lando Norris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lando_Norris \"Lando Norris\").[[275]](#cite_note-279)\n\n## Media\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8e/Bristol._Whiteladies_Rd._BBC_Broadcasting_House.jpg/250px-Bristol._Whiteladies_Rd._BBC_Broadcasting_House.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nBristol is home to the regional headquarters of [BBC West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_West \"BBC West\") and the BBC Natural History Unit based at Broadcasting House, which produces television, radio and online content with a [natural history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_history \"Natural history\") or [wildlife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife \"Wildlife\") theme. The city has a long association with [David Attenborough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Attenborough \"David Attenborough\")'s authored documentaries, including *[Life on Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_on_Earth_(TV_series) \"Life on Earth (TV series)\")*,[[276]](#cite_note-280) *[The Blue Planet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Blue_Planet \"The Blue Planet\")* and *[Planet Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planet_Earth_(franchise) \"Planet Earth (franchise)\")*.\n\nThe BBC announced in 2021 that it was moving the production of many of its programmes from Broadcasting House to [Finzels Reach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finzels_Reach \"Finzels Reach\") in the centre of Bristol.[[277]](#cite_note-281)\n\nBristol has two daily newspapers, the *[Western Daily Press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Daily_Press \"Western Daily Press\")* and the *[Bristol Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Post \"Bristol Post\")* (both owned by [Reach plc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reach_plc \"Reach plc\")); and a Bristol edition of the free [*Metro*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_(British_newspaper) \"Metro (British newspaper)\") newspaper (owned by [DMGT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DMGT \"DMGT\")). *[The Bristol Cable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Bristol_Cable \"The Bristol Cable\")* specialises in investigative journalism with a quarterly print edition and website.\n\nAardman Animations is a Bristol-based animation studio, known for the characters [Wallace & Gromit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallace_&_Gromit \"Wallace & Gromit\") and [Morph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morph_(animation) \"Morph (animation)\").\n\nLocal radio stations, including [BBC Radio Bristol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_Radio_Bristol \"BBC Radio Bristol\"), [Heart West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heart_West \"Heart West\"), [Greatest Hits Radio Bristol & The South West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greatest_Hits_Radio_Bristol_&_The_South_West \"Greatest Hits Radio Bristol & The South West\"), [Hits Radio Bristol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_FM_(Bristol) \"Sam FM (Bristol)\"), [Kiss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiss_(UK_radio_station) \"Kiss (UK radio station)\"), and [BCfm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BCfm \"BCfm\"), a community based station. Television programmes produced in the city include *[Points West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_Points_West \"BBC Points West\")*, Endemol productions such as *[Deal or No Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deal_or_No_Deal \"Deal or No Deal\")*, *[The Crystal Maze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Crystal_Maze \"The Crystal Maze\")*, *[Tipping Point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tipping_Point_(game_show) \"Tipping Point (game show)\")* and *[ITV News West Country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ITV_News_West_Country \"ITV News West Country\")*. Coming-of-age drama *[Skins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skins_(British_TV_series) \"Skins (British TV series)\")* and supernatural comedy-drama *[Being Human](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Being_Human_(British_TV_series) \"Being Human (British TV series)\")* were also shot in the city. Bristol was the former home of long-running BBC medical soap opera *[Casualty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casualty_(TV_series) \"Casualty (TV series)\")* until 2011. In October 2018, [Channel 4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_4 \"Channel 4\") announced that Bristol would be home to one of its 'Creative Hubs', as part of their move to produce more content outside of London.[[278]](#cite_note-282)\n\nPublishers in the city have included 18th-century Bristolian Joseph Cottle, who helped introduce [Romanticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism \"Romanticism\") by publishing the works of William Wordsworth and Samuel Taylor Coleridge.[[279]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMadden1972419-283) During the 19th century, J.W. Arrowsmith published the Victorian comedies *[Three Men in a Boat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Men_in_a_Boat \"Three Men in a Boat\")* (by [Jerome K. Jerome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerome_K._Jerome \"Jerome K. Jerome\")) and *[The Diary of a Nobody](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Diary_of_a_Nobody \"The Diary of a Nobody\")* by [George](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Grossmith \"George Grossmith\") and [Weedon Grossmith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weedon_Grossmith \"Weedon Grossmith\").[[280]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJerome1889-284) The contemporary Redcliffe Press has published over 200 books covering all aspects of the city.[[281]](#cite_note-285) Bristol is home to [YouTube](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YouTube \"YouTube\") video developers and stylists [The Yogscast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Yogscast \"The Yogscast\"), with founders Simon Lane and Lewis Brindley having moved their operations from [Reading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reading,_Berkshire \"Reading, Berkshire\") to Bristol in 2012.[[282]](#cite_note-286)\n\n## Education\n\n![ A Palladian style nineteenth century stone building with a large colonnaded porch. In front a large metal statue on a pedestal and fountains with decorations.](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Victoria_Rooms_%28750px%29.jpg/250px-Victoria_Rooms_%28750px%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nBristol has two major institutions of higher education: the [University of Bristol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Bristol \"University of Bristol\"), a [redbrick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redbrick \"Redbrick\") chartered in 1909;[[283]](#cite_note-287) and the [University of the West of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_the_West_of_England \"University of the West of England\"), opened as Bristol [Polytechnic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_of_technology \"Institute of technology\") in 1969, which became a university in 1992.[[284]](#cite_note-288) [The University of Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_Law \"The University of Law\") also has a campus in the city. Bristol has two [further education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Further_education \"Further education\") institutions ([City of Bristol College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Bristol_College \"City of Bristol College\") and [South Gloucestershire and Stroud College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Gloucestershire_and_Stroud_College \"South Gloucestershire and Stroud College\")) and two [theological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theological \"Theological\") colleges: [Trinity College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity_College,_Bristol \"Trinity College, Bristol\"), and [Bristol Baptist College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Baptist_College \"Bristol Baptist College\").\n\nBristol has 129 [infant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant_school \"Infant school\"), [junior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Junior_school \"Junior school\") and primary schools,[[285]](#cite_note-289)\n[17 secondary schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_schools_in_Bristol \"List of schools in Bristol\"),[[286]](#cite_note-290) and three learning centres. After a section of north London, Bristol has England's second-highest number of [private school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_schools_in_the_United_Kingdom \"Private schools in the United Kingdom\") places.[[287]](#cite_note-curtis-291) Independent schools in Bristol include [Clifton College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clifton_College \"Clifton College\"), [Clifton High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clifton_High_School,_Bristol \"Clifton High School, Bristol\"), [Badminton School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badminton_School \"Badminton School\"), [Bristol Grammar School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Grammar_School \"Bristol Grammar School\"), [Queen Elizabeth's Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Elizabeth's_Hospital \"Queen Elizabeth's Hospital\") (the only all-boys school) and the [Redmaids' School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redmaids'_School \"Redmaids' School\") (founded in 1634 by John Whitson, which claims to be England's oldest girls' school).[[288]](#cite_note-292)\n\n![A tall stone nineteenth-century building with shields on the visible sides and a pepperpot upper storey. In front, traffic and pedestrians on a busy street.](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/51/University_of_bristol_tower_after_cleaning_arp.jpg/250px-University_of_bristol_tower_after_cleaning_arp.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\n[Playing Out](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Playing_Out \"Playing Out\") is a [community interest company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_interest_company \"Community interest company\") founded in 2009 in Bristol by Alice Ferguson and Amy Rose.[[289]](#cite_note-293) It supports residents to organise temporary street closures creating [play streets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Play_street \"Play street\") so that children can play safely outside.[[290]](#cite_note-294)[[291]](#cite_note-295)\n\n### Science city\n\nIn 2005, [Chancellor of the Exchequer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer \"Chancellor of the Exchequer\") [Gordon Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gordon_Brown \"Gordon Brown\") named Bristol one of six English 'science cities',[[292]](#cite_note-296)\nand a £300 million science park was planned at [Emersons Green](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emersons_Green \"Emersons Green\").[[293]](#cite_note-297) Research is conducted at the two universities, the [Bristol Royal Infirmary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Royal_Infirmary \"Bristol Royal Infirmary\") and [Southmead Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southmead_Hospital \"Southmead Hospital\"), and science outreach is practised at [We The Curious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/We_The_Curious \"We The Curious\"), the Bristol Zoo, the [Bristol Festival of Nature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Festival_of_Nature \"Bristol Festival of Nature\") and the CREATE Centre.[[294]](#cite_note-298)\n\nThe city has produced a number of scientists, including 19th-century chemist [Humphry Davy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humphry_Davy \"Humphry Davy\")[[295]](#cite_note-299) (who worked in [Hotwells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hotwells \"Hotwells\")). Physicist [Paul Dirac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Dirac \"Paul Dirac\") (from [Bishopston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bishopston,_Bristol \"Bishopston, Bristol\")) received the 1933 Nobel Prize for his contributions to [quantum mechanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_mechanics \"Quantum mechanics\").[[296]](#cite_note-300) [Cecil Frank Powell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cecil_Frank_Powell \"Cecil Frank Powell\") was the Melvill Wills Professor of Physics at the University of Bristol when he received the 1950 Nobel Prize for, among other discoveries, his photographic method of studying nuclear processes. [Colin Pillinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colin_Pillinger \"Colin Pillinger\")[[297]](#cite_note-301) was the planetary scientist behind the [Beagle 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beagle_2 \"Beagle 2\") project, and neuropsychologist [Richard Gregory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Gregory \"Richard Gregory\") founded the Exploratory (a hands-on science centre which was the predecessor of At-Bristol/We The Curious).[[298]](#cite_note-302)\n\nInitiatives such as the [Flying Start Challenge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flying_Start_Challenge \"Flying Start Challenge\") encourage an interest in science and engineering in Bristol secondary-school pupils; links with aerospace companies impart technical information and advance student understanding of design.[[299]](#cite_note-303)\nThe [Bloodhound LSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloodhound_LSR \"Bloodhound LSR\") project to break the [land speed record](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_speed_record \"Land speed record\") is based at the Bloodhound Technology Centre on the city's harbourside.[[300]](#cite_note-304)\n\n## Transport\n\n### tent=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/BSicon_exKDST2.svg/20px-BSicon_exKDST2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/BSicon_xABZg3.svg/20px-BSicon_xABZg3.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/BSicon_exSTR.svg/20px-BSicon_exSTR.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/BSicon_HST.svg/20px-BSicon_HST.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/37/BSicon_KDST1.svg/20px-BSicon_KDST1.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/BSicon_STRc4.svg/20px-BSicon_STRc4.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/BSicon_exABZg%2B4.svg/20px-BSicon_exABZg%2B4.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/BSicon_exCONTf.svg/20px-BSicon_exCONTf.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/BSicon_TUNNEL1.svg/20px-BSicon_TUNNEL1.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/BSicon_exHST.svg/20px-BSicon_exHST.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bf/BSicon_eHST.svg/20px-BSicon_eHST.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/BSicon_exdKSTR2.svg/20px-BSicon_exdKSTR2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/44/BSicon_exdHST.svg/20px-BSicon_exdHST.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/BSicon_excSTRc3.svg/20px-BSicon_excSTRc3.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/BSicon_CONTf.svg/20px-BSicon_CONTf.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d6/BSicon_exdSTRc1.svg/20px-BSicon_exdSTRc1.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/BSicon_exdSTR.svg/20px-BSicon_exdSTR.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/BSicon_exlvHST-.svg/20px-BSicon_exlvHST-.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3d/BSicon_exdKSTR%2B4.svg/20px-BSicon_exdKSTR%2B4.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\n|  |\n| --- |\n| [Weston, Clevedon and](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weston,_Clevedon_and_Portishead_Light_Railway \"Weston, Clevedon and Portishead Light Railway\") |\n| [Portishead Light Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weston,_Clevedon_and_Portishead_Light_Railway \"Weston, Clevedon and Portishead Light Railway\") |\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/BSicon_exmABZg%2Bl.svg/20px-BSicon_exmABZg%2Bl.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/BSicon_uexCONTfq.svg/20px-BSicon_uexCONTfq.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/BSicon_exHST.svg/20px-BSicon_exHST.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/BSicon_exKDSTe.svg/20px-BSicon_exKDSTe.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nBristol has two principal railway stations. [Bristol Temple Meads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Temple_Meads \"Bristol Temple Meads\"), near the city centre, has [Great Western Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Western_Railway_(train_operating_company) \"Great Western Railway (train operating company)\") services which include high-speed trains to [London Paddington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paddington_railway_station \"Paddington railway station\") and local, regional and [CrossCountry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CrossCountry \"CrossCountry\") trains. [Bristol Parkway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Parkway_railway_station \"Bristol Parkway railway station\"), north of the city in Gloucestershire, but within the conurbation, has high-speed Great Western Railway services to [Swansea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swansea_railway_station \"Swansea railway station\"), [Cardiff Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardiff_Central_railway_station \"Cardiff Central railway station\") and London Paddington, and CrossCountry services reaching [Birmingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham_New_Street_railway_station \"Birmingham New Street railway station\"), [Manchester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchester_Piccadilly_railway_station \"Manchester Piccadilly railway station\") and [Edinburgh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edinburgh_Waverley_railway_station \"Edinburgh Waverley railway station\"). There are scheduled coach links to most major UK cities.[[301]](#cite_note-progrep-305)\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Bristol_Temple_Meads_station_%286466232797%29.jpg/250px-Bristol_Temple_Meads_station_%286466232797%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nBristol's principal surviving suburban railway is the [Severn Beach Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Severn_Beach_Line \"Severn Beach Line\") to Avonmouth and [Severn Beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Severn_Beach \"Severn Beach\"). Although [Portishead Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portishead_Railway \"Portishead Railway\")'s passenger service was a casualty of the [Beeching cuts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beeching_cuts \"Beeching cuts\"), freight service to the Royal Portbury Dock was restored from 2000 to 2002. The [MetroWest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MetroWest_(Bristol) \"MetroWest (Bristol)\") scheme, formerly known as The Greater Bristol Metro, proposes to increase the city's rail capacity[[302]](#cite_note-306) including the restoration of a further 3 mi (5 km) of track on the line to [Portishead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portishead,_Somerset \"Portishead, Somerset\") (a [dormitory town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dormitory_town \"Dormitory town\") with one connecting road), and a further commuter rail line from Bristol Temple Meads to [Henbury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henbury,_Bristol \"Henbury, Bristol\"), on an [existing freight line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henbury_Loop_Line \"Henbury Loop Line\"). Following numerous delays, the two lines are due to be opened in 2026.[[303]](#cite_note-307)[[304]](#cite_note-308)\n\n### Roads\n\nThe M4 motorway connects the city on an east–west axis from London to [West Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Wales \"West Wales\"), and the M5 is a north–southwest axis from Birmingham to Exeter. The [M49 motorway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M49_motorway \"M49 motorway\") is a shortcut between the M5 in the south and the M4 [Severn Crossing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Severn_Crossing \"Second Severn Crossing\") in the west, and the [M32](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M32_motorway \"M32 motorway\") is a spur from the M4 to the city centre.[[301]](#cite_note-progrep-305) The [Portway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portway,_Bristol \"Portway, Bristol\") connects the M5 to the city centre, and was the most expensive road in Britain when opened in 1926.[[305]](#cite_note-309)[[306]](#cite_note-310)\n\n![Royal Portbury Dock.](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Somerset_north_portbury_dock.jpg/250px-Somerset_north_portbury_dock.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nPrivate car use is high in the city, leading to traffic congestion costing an estimated £350 million per year.[[307]](#cite_note-ltp06.1-311)\nBristol allows motorcycles to use most of the city's bus lanes and provides secure, free parking for them.[[308]](#cite_note-312) In 2022, Bristol implemented a [Clean Air Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clean_Air_Zone \"Clean Air Zone\") to reduce pollution by charging the most polluting vehicles to enter the city centre, with the money raised reinvested in transport.[[309]](#cite_note-bp-20220706-313)\n\n### Public transport\n\nPublic transport in the city consists primarily of a [First West of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_West_of_England \"First West of England\") bus network. Other providers are Abus,[[310]](#cite_note-314) [Stagecoach West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stagecoach_West \"Stagecoach West\"), and [Stagecoach South West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stagecoach_South_West \"Stagecoach South West\").[[311]](#cite_note-315)[[312]](#cite_note-316) Bristol's bus service has been criticised as unreliable and expensive, and in 2005 FirstGroup was fined for delays and safety violations.[[313]](#cite_note-317)[[314]](#cite_note-318)\n\nAlthough the city council has included a [light rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_rail \"Light rail\") system in its [local transport plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_transport_plan \"Local transport plan\") since 2000, it has been unsuccessful in funding the project.[[315]](#cite_note-319) Instead, Bristol has a network of five [bus rapid transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_rapid_transit \"Bus rapid transit\") routes, named [MetroBus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MetroBus_(Bristol) \"MetroBus (Bristol)\"), which in part run on dedicated infrastructure. The Metrobus project aimed to provide a faster and more reliable service than standard buses, improving transport infrastructure and reducing congestion,[[316]](#cite_note-320) but has been criticised as a limited success because the routes are not fully segregated from other traffic.[[317]](#cite_note-321) The first routes opened in 2018,[[318]](#cite_note-322) and as of 2025, they connect the city centre with [Emersons Green](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emersons_Green \"Emersons Green\"), [Bristol Parkway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Parkway \"Bristol Parkway\"), [Cribbs Causeway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cribbs_Causeway \"Cribbs Causeway\"), [Hengrove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hengrove \"Hengrove\"), and a [park and ride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Park_and_ride_bus_services_in_the_United_Kingdom \"Park and ride bus services in the United Kingdom\") near [Long Ashton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_Ashton \"Long Ashton\").[[319]](#cite_note-323)\n\nThree [park and ride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Park_and_ride \"Park and ride\") sites serve Bristol.[[320]](#cite_note-324)\nThe city centre has water transport operated by [Bristol Ferry Boats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Ferry_Boats \"Bristol Ferry Boats\"), [Bristol Packet Boat Trips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Packet_Boat_Trips \"Bristol Packet Boat Trips\") and Number Seven Boat Trips, providing leisure and commuter service in the harbour.[[321]](#cite_note-325)\n\n### Cycling\n\nBristol was designated as England's first \"cycling city\" in 2008 and one of England's 12 \"Cycling demonstration\" areas.[[322]](#cite_note-326) It is home to [Sustrans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustrans \"Sustrans\"), the sustainable transport charity. The [Bristol and Bath Railway Path](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_and_Bath_Railway_Path \"Bristol and Bath Railway Path\") links it to Bath, and was the first part of the [National Cycle Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Cycle_Network \"National Cycle Network\"). The city also has urban cycle routes and links with National Cycle Network routes to the rest of the Country. Cycling trips increased by 21% from 2001 to 2005.[[307]](#cite_note-ltp06.1-311)\n\n### Air\n\n![An aerial view of an airport with one main runway, car parks on the left and right, and aircraft parked outside terminal buildings on the right.](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Bristol_airport_overview.jpg/250px-Bristol_airport_overview.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nIn 2023, [Bristol Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Airport \"Bristol Airport\") (BRS), located in neighbouring [North Somerset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Somerset \"North Somerset\"), was ranked the eighth [busiest airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Busiest_airports_in_the_United_Kingdom_by_total_passenger_traffic \"Busiest airports in the United Kingdom by total passenger traffic\") in the United Kingdom. It handled 9.9 million passengers, an 14% increase compared with 2018.[[323]](#cite_note-caa2023-327)\n\n## Twin towns – sister cities\n\n![The walls and tower of an old ruined church set in a paved area and surrounded by a park. On the left is water with some pontoons moored and in the background office blocks, streets and church spires.](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/Castle.park.bristol.arp.jpg/250px-Castle.park.bristol.arp.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nBristol was among the first cities to adopt [town twinning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Town_twinning \"Town twinning\") after World War II.[[324]](#cite_note-328)[[325]](#cite_note-329) Twin towns
